## Judge each requirement on

1. **Checkable.** Can its *Fails if* actually fail? Name one concrete case that breaks it, or say it cannot fail.
2. **One thing.** Does it describe one event, or two that should be split?
3. **Units and weak words.** Does every comparison say what it counts? Any vague word (enough, about, period, once)?
4. **Source.** Is the label (v / d / proposed) right? Open the cited id: does the source say what is claimed?
5. **Consistency.** Does it contradict the vision, another requirement, or other text in `CACHES.md` (the rules, the
   5W1H tables, the routing tables, the example numbers)? Quote both lines.
6. **Gaps.** What must be true for the caches to work that no requirement says? Do not invent numbers; say what is
   missing.

source that shows it (quoted), severity (high, medium, low), and a proposed fix in one or two sentences.

### Gaps

### What holds

### Not checked

# CLAUDE.md — Ancient Games

## The name

**The Ancient Games** — named by EJ, 2026-09-06. Its principles come from two poles: **孫子兵法** (ancient Chinese
strategy) on one side and **game theory** on the other, with **modern military doctrine** and **complexity science**
in between.

## What this folder is for

This folder is EJ's place for designing dynamic workflows for agent work, using the knowledge kept here.

The Ancient Games is one framework for agent work: the workflow-design method (`.claude/skills/workflow-design/`)
is the part that turns an incoming task into a designed workflow, and `ancient_games/` is the part that referees
the work, with the referee's findings feeding back into the method.

The method's skill is linked into both harness roots so it works in every project —
`~/.agents/skills/workflow-design` (the user-global root the DeepSeek Harness and Codex scan) and
`~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` (Claude Code). The knowledge below is what that skill uses.

Designing a workflow means deciding, for a given task: which kinds of agents to use, what runs in sequence and what
runs in parallel, what repeats (with its limit and stop condition), who decides who works next, how agents pass
information and where they must not, and how each result is verified. Runnable workflows live in
`.claude/workflows/`.

When EJ brings a task or a question about a workflow, the job is to help design that workflow from this knowledge.
Work on the referee code (`ancient_games/`) serves that purpose; it is not the goal by itself.

## The knowledge here

- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` — the method for turning a challenge into a prompt file or a workflow design:
  understand the challenge (objective, scope, six whys, category), readiness (with a check of the product's
  blueprint), algorithm first, list the unclear spots, clear pieces as loops, then the graph; sequential first;
  state-file driven. Templates in `docs/workflow-templates/`. Runnable form:
  `.claude/workflows/intake.js` (args `{challenge, runId}`); each run writes to `runs/<runId>/`.
- `docs/TASK_TYPES.md` — the default execution pattern, engine, check and sabotage per task category, seeded
  combination pipelines, and the 20% challenger rule with `docs/TASK_TYPES_LEDGER.md`. The skill's
  `scripts/design_gate.py` parses it — this file is the table's only copy (n=0 until the ledger fills).
- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_DIAGRAM.md` — the same algorithm as diagrams: the whole flow, the blueprint check, what the
  method takes from the referee code, and the mapping from method section numbers to the skill's steps.
- `docs/EXECUTOR_KINDS.md` — who can run a node (method 3.1 / 3.6): the engines (Claude subagent, Codex, Antigravity `agy`, Qwen, opencode), what each is assigned, how it is called, its read-only form and structured output, and its
  default model and effort. A node's engine choice reads this. n=0; *reported* versus *verified* marked throughout.
- `.claude/skills/workflow-design/` — the method packaged as a skill. `SKILL.md` is the packaging and the trigger:
  its frontmatter `description` is what makes an agent decide to use it, and its body is the method as eight steps.
  Its method and templates are links into `docs/`, so there is one copy of each. It is the source of truth;
  `~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` is a link to it, which makes it available in every project.
- `20260919-state.md` — what Ancient Games can and cannot decide today about graph, loop, swarm, sequential,
  parallel and communication patterns, and the proposed direction. Start here for workflow design.
- `ancient_games/` — the referee: the part of the framework that referees agent work. Fixed rules, no LLM calls, a
  journal of every decision. Five stages: Gate (how many agents), Guard (which actions need approval), Corroborate (how many independent
  sources a claim needs), Filter (which candidates survive), Prove (is the plan cleared).
- `PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` — map of the code, how a run works, open findings F1–F11.
- `SPEC.md`, `DECISIONS.md` — the referee's contract. Only EJ changes these.
- `docs/` — design documents and ablations: what was tried, what held, what is marked n=1 and not to be built on.
- `.claude/workflows/enhance-ancient-games.js` — a worked example of a designed workflow: dependencies between
  segments, bounded verify/repair loops, independent verifiers.

## Working with EJ here

- Answer the question that was asked, in plain language, before offering options or plans.
- Discuss first. Do not plan or run anything large until EJ says so.
- Tests: `env -u NO_COLOR python3 -m pytest -q`

### When EJ states a rule, a requirement or an algorithm

Asked for by EJ, 2026-10-05: *"each time when I told you something, you will eval my statement or algo, and ask me
what I missing, and we complete it."*

**Evaluate it, do not just apply it.** Run the statement through the **`requirement-check` skill** — load it
(`skill requirement-check`) or read `~/skills/requirement-check/SKILL.md` — and say which of the eight it passes and
fails. Then **ask EJ the failing ones** and write the completed version together.

The eight, by name: **source · one thing · unit · can it fail · weak words · level · basis · links.** Their
definitions live in the skill — one copy, not four.

**Two rules for me, learned the hard way on 2026-10-05:**

- **Never fill a gap with my own value.** If the unit, threshold or test is missing, it goes to EJ as an open
  question — inventing it is how rules become weird later. Recorded in the register as 11.1/11.2.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No "halves", no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs unless the person
  said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop.

- **Ask only what changes the shape.** A question the template already answers, or that has an obvious default, is a
  question I should not ask — it is "never add structure" done to *questions* instead of facts. Three of four
  questions I asked EJ on 2026-10-06 (a large-batch cap, "may the worker refuse", the list's destination) were
  invented problems; only one changed the template. State the sensible default and move on.

### Who is this for — and who fills it in?

EJ, 2026-10-05: *"have to remember we doing things for who."* **Every artifact has one maker.** A return template is
the worker's output, so every field in it is written by the worker; a verdict is the human's decision, and it does not
share the worker's form. Before adding a field, a step or a rule, name **who fills it** — if the answer is "someone
else, later", it is a different artifact, not a field on this one.

This is the "one thing" question applied to *people* instead of *facts*: a form that two different people fill at two
different times is two forms.

### Quotes, labels, and who wrote the check

EJ, 2026-10-11: *"please add these to your memory"* — after one session in which I misled him three times. All three
are **SHOULD** (a default, overridable with a stated reason): a presentation rule is method, and method does not
earn MUST (RFC 2119 §6). Run through `requirement-check`: quoting and labelling failed **weak words**, naming the
check failed **one thing** and was split in two; the trigger and the grain are EJ's answers, not mine.

- **Quote when a source's own words are the evidence, and when a list is compressed into a count.** *"Current agent
  memory splits short-term from long-term three ways"* invited EJ to read "an agent has three memory systems"; the
  report's own line — tiers with promotion, file placement, validity windows — carries the meaning. Give the
  source's line, or the count with its items in the same breath.
- **Label a conclusion as *quoted* (with its id), *my reading*, or *not measured*.** It bites on a conclusion,
  verdict, answer or summary line, never on narrative. *"The research was never the problem"* was my reading of a
  structural check, stated as a fact.
- **Name who wrote a check** before resting a conclusion on it.
- **Name what that check does not establish.** A check I wrote and then passed is not independent evidence: the
  acceptance gate's item 6 ended as a script of mine, so "6 of 6 items pass" without that sentence reads stronger
  than it is.

Basis: n=1 each, 2026-10-11 — `runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph/run-1-report.md`,
`runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/run-2-report.md`, and the session thread. What **accepted** means is defined
once, in `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` Appendix A.

### Evidence lives in the project, not `/tmp`

EJ, 2026-10-06. The pass-back `evidence:` path points into `runs/<run-id>/` — **SHOULD**, not MUST: a worker may
leave evidence elsewhere with a written reason, but the default is the run's folder. **Basis: n=1** — the
code-graph-tool run's optimize node wrote its "old-vs-new" proof to `/tmp`, it was deleted, and the fix became
unverifiable. When evidence needs a persistent home, **suggest `runs/<run-id>/` and get the user's agreement** — do
not pick a path silently. Scratch in `/tmp` is fine; it is the *refer-back* artifact that must not stay there.

---
name: requirement-check
description: Evaluate a rule, requirement, threshold or algorithm against requirement-quality criteria before applying or writing it down, and ask the person which criteria it fails. Use when someone states a rule, requirement, constraint, threshold, algorithm or design guideline; when writing or editing a normative line in a document, plan or config; or when a rule reads as arbitrary, unattributed or impossible to check.
---

# Requirement check

A general standard (RFC 2119 / 8174), installed globally — not part of the Ancient Games framework. The
worked failure in `meaningful-names` cites the Ancient Games as one example.

A short gate between **hearing a rule** and **acting on it**. It exists because rules get written down that nobody
can trace, that count two different things at once, or that nothing can ever fail — and the cost lands months later,
when nobody can say whether the rule still means anything.

Run it when someone states a rule, and run it on yourself before writing a normative line into any document.

## The eight questions

Ask them of **one statement**. Say which it passes and which it fails, then **ask the person about the failures** —
do not fill the gaps yourself.

1. **Source.** Who said this, and where? Can you point to the exact line? *"Mine"* is a valid answer. Unlabelled is
   not.
2. **One thing.** An "and", an "or", a "/", or a second number means it is **two** statements. Split them, and give
   each its own source.
3. **Unit.** Every number says what it counts. Attempts or hours? Roles, or processes running at once? Bytes or
   files?
4. **Can it fail.** Name one concrete input that would make it fail. `TBD`, `—`, `0` and empty must be among the
   failing inputs. If you cannot name one, it is not checkable — it is a wish.
5. **Weak words.** *like, about, appropriate, as needed, logical, fast, enough, reasonable, only has to, TBD.* Replace
   each with a value, **or mark the value open**. Never invent the number.
6. **Level.** Is it **MUST** (a gate that stops work), **SHOULD** (a default that may be overridden with a stated
   reason), or **guidance**? A safety bound earns MUST. **A method does not** — see the RFC note below.
7. **Basis.** Measured, quoted from a source, or a guess? A guess is fine **if it is labelled** — `n=0`, with when it
   will be looked at again.
8. **Links.** Does it claim a relation to another rule — *"half of"*, *"pairs with"*, *"the same as"*, *"the runtime
   part"*? The person probably did not say that. **A link is its own claim and needs its own source.**

## Two rules for the agent

- **Never fill a gap with your own value.** A missing unit, threshold or test goes back as a question. Inventing it
  is exactly how a rule becomes unattributable later, and the invention is indistinguishable from the original by
  the time anyone notices.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No halves, no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs, no named phases,
  unless the person said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop. If a relationship between two rules is
  wanted, that is a separate claim with its own evidence.

## Two verified facts, worth quoting

- **RFC 2119 §6, "Guidance in the use of these Imperatives":** imperatives *"must be used with care and sparingly…
  they MUST only be used where it is actually required… they must not be used to try to impose a particular method."*
  Most design rules are method. Method does not earn MUST.
- **RFC 8174 (updates 2119):** the key words *"have the meanings specified herein only when they are in all
  capitals."* Lowercase *must* and *should* carry no defined requirement meaning. If nothing marks which lines are
  requirements, a reader cannot tell rules from explanation.

## How to answer

Evaluate first, in a table or a short list — **pass / FAIL per question**. Then ask the failing ones as questions,
one line each, with the options if you can see them. Two or three real questions beat eight formalities.

Do not report a rule as complete when you chose its missing value. Say which values you left open.

## A worked failure

> *"At most 5 subagent roles per run and at most 3 running at once."*

Four of the eight fail: **Source** — nobody could name one; **One thing** — a count of roles *and* a count of
concurrent processes; **Unit** — two different units, one word ("subagent") used for both; **Level** — a method
preference written as a cap. The sentence would not parse, and it took four days to find out why. The checklist
catches it in thirty seconds.

The same checklist, run on *"No single call runs longer than the ceiling: 8 hours"*, passes all eight — because it
names who set it, counts one thing, uses a unit, and some input (a 40-hour plan) makes it fail.

## Limits

The quality characteristics behind these questions (necessary, appropriate, unambiguous, complete, singular,
feasible, verifiable, correct, conforming) come from requirements-engineering sources — IEEE 830, ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148
and the INCOSE *Guide to Writing Requirements* — which were **not read** when this skill was written: they are
paywalled or blocked, and the list is recalled rather than verified. The two RFC points above **were** read, and the
quotes are checked. Treat the rest as a good working list, not as a citation.

Origin: `docs/research/20261005-requirement-statements/FINDINGS.md` in the Ancient Games project, 2026-10-05.

 ### Five whys
 
-1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code: what an edit touches, and whether the code an
-   entry covers has changed.
-2. **Why would memory need that?** For the drift check (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 75: mark a decision or fact whose
-   code changed with no earlier change to the entry) and for "which entries cover the files I am editing".
-3. **Why is git enough for both?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
-   to path (`G-F14`). One `git log` from the entry's verified revision lists the changes to its `covers:` paths, in
-   0.00–0.01 s on this repo (`G-F12`; code-graph node §6). Git is the record itself, so it cannot drift.
-4. **Why not add a graph for precision anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that git does not
-   have (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but
-   it covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
-   The precision it buys, a one-line comment flagging a whole file, can be had by parsing only the changed file with
-   Python's `ast` (code-graph node §5, option E). The deeper question, "is this entry still true", belongs to the
-   background subagent that compares entries with the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` lines 68–73), not to a model-free
-   script.
-5. **Why might it be needed later?** Any one of the three conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:
+1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code, such as what an edit touches.
+2. **Why would memory need that?** For "which entries cover the files I am editing" (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd4.1). The
+   drift check it once also served was removed by EJ on 2026-10-12.
+3. **Why is a text search enough?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
+   to path (`G-F14`), so finding the entries that cover a file is a search over `covers:` lines.
+4. **Why not add a graph anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that a text search does not have
+   (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but it
+   covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
+   Whether an entry is still true is answered by reading it against the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd5), not by a
+   graph.
+5. **Why might it be needed later?** Either of two conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:

     1	# Caches — product vision statement
     2	
     3	> **Status: settled** (accepted by EJ on 2026-10-12). EJ's words: *"ok the vision is confirm"*. Drafted 2026-10-12
     4	> by the COO from the research in
     5	> `runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/` and `runs/20261011-project-memory-engines/RECOMMENDATION.md`; rewritten the
     6	> same day at EJ's direction — more direct, fewer hedges.
     7	>
     8	> **Drawn from:** `docs/memory-system/CACHES.md`, which carries the core requirements (Build / Keep / Read) this
     9	> vision serves. This file holds the vision only, so each has one copy (`docs/workflow-templates/blueprint.md` keeps
    10	> vision and requirements in separate section files).
    11	>
    12	> **Whose it is.** Per the blueprint template, the product vision is EJ's — *"an agent may only write down what EJ
    13	> has said"*. This was drafted from the research at his request and settled by him on 2026-10-12.
    14	>
    15	> **Changed 2026-10-12:** the code graph removed at EJ's direction (*"code graph should have it's own project"*);
    16	> its draft is `docs/code-graph/CODE_GRAPH.md`. The problem now names both ways it shows up, one per cache — the
    17	> vector reason was missing (EJ: *"ok write it into the vision"*). "How we know it works" now tests that the right
    18	> entry is found for less (B1.1, B1.2), not only that a rebuild repeats; and the rebuild's "no model call" now
    19	> excepts the vector embedding step, as B5.1 does (EJ: *"ok write it into the vision"*).
    20	
    21	The caches are two optional indexes over the project's markdown memory — full-text search and vector search. Both
    22	live in `project-memory/.cache/`, both are gitignored, and both are rebuilt from the files. Neither is needed today.
    23	
    24	**Who it is for.** An agent retrieving an entry on demand. Not the human reviewer, who reads the entries themselves,
    25	and not EJ, who sets the triggers.
    26	
    27	**The problem it solves.** The memory is markdown in git, and today `INDEX.md` plus grep finds what an agent needs —
    28	at 10,000 entries a grep takes 0.038–0.06 s (`S-F13`). As the memory grows, that stops being enough in two ways:
    29	- **Too many entries — full-text search.** `INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry under its size limit,
    30	  so the one-line scan of everything is gone; and grep returns unranked matches, where every hit an agent opens
    31	  costs context (`S-F1`).
    32	- **Different words — vector search.** The agent's words differ from the entry's, so `INDEX.md`, grep and
    33	  full-text search all miss an entry that exists (`V-F13`).
    34	
    35	The caches exist to keep finding the right entry cheap when that happens — and not before.
    36	
    37	**What it will never do.**
    38	- It is never the source of truth. The markdown is, always, and a cache holds nothing the files do not (`M4`, `M6`).
    39	- It is never patched incrementally. It is rebuilt whole or not at all — a live index drifted 4.3% of its edges from
    40	  a clean rebuild with every integrity check passing (`G-F5`).
    41	- It is never read at session start (`M18`).
    42	- It is never built before its own trigger fires. Every trigger is an observable comparison, and the measurement
    43	  decides it, not a number set in advance (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"this is design guide, how can I give you a number?"*).
    44	- It never becomes a second thing to keep in sync: delete the folder and memory is unaffected.
    45	
    46	**How we know it works.** Two tests, both on the project's own recorded questions.
    47	- **It finds the right entry, for less.** Full-text search reaches the right entry after reading less than
    48	  `INDEX.md` plus grep, with no more misses (B1.1). Vector search, run on the recorded misses, finds entries
    49	  full-text did not, and does no worse on the other questions (B1.2).
    50	- **It holds nothing of its own.** Delete the cache, rebuild it from the files, and the same query returns the
    51	  same entries, with no model call except the vector embedding step (`M6`, B5.1; a 10,000-entry FTS rebuild takes
    52	  0.53 s, `S-F13`).
    53	
    54	One caveat travels with every number here: none of it has been measured on a real `project-memory/` corpus,
    55	because that corpus does not exist yet (`S-F5`, `V-F11`).

    40	## The answer first
    41	
    42	None of the three caches is needed today **for memory entries**. `project-memory/` does not exist yet, so nothing has
    43	been measured on it. My reading of the research is that at the sizes measured elsewhere, `INDEX.md`, grep and git
    44	answer the questions each cache would answer. Each cache below has a
    45	**trigger**: a condition that can be observed and that makes it worth building. None of them is built before its
    46	trigger fires.
    47	
    48	The caches are for **memory entries** only: that is the vision EJ confirmed on 2026-10-12. The measured section below
    49	on code and docs is kept as evidence; it is outside the vision, and its proposals to widen the vision were not adopted.
    50	
    51	This follows the design's own rule: `.cache/` is optional, gitignored and rebuilt from the files, and its absence
    52	must not stop anyone reading memory (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`; recommendation M4, M6). It also follows the repo's own
    53	history of building graphs nobody queried (`docs/KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md` §1, quoted in `G-F21`).
    54	
    55	## When to use which store
    56	
    57	The two tables below cover two different corpora and are kept apart. **Part 1 is for memory entries**
    58	(`project-memory/`), reasoned from the research and the run record. **Part 2 is for code and docs**, measured on this
    59	repo. In both parts a cache is derived and optional and is never the source of truth; once its trigger fires it is a
    60	cheaper way to reach the same entry.
    61	
    62	### Part 1 — memory entries (corpus: `project-memory/`)
    63	
    64	At query time the memory is answered by the files — `INDEX.md`, grep, git, or reading the entry — and the files are
    65	the source of truth, not the cache. The table below merges three engines asked independently, one store
    66	each: `claude` on the code graph, `codex` on the vector index, `opencode` on SQLite FTS5. The full 21-row table and
    67	the 30-row cost table, with every disagreement kept, are in `runs/20261012-caches-enhance/DECISION-RULES.md` — the
    68	run record. **This file is the live copy.**
    69	
    70	**The rule.** **SQLite FTS5** is for a free-text question whose words are likely in the entry text, once `INDEX.md`
    71	can no longer list every active entry under its size limit and ranked results with snippets save opened files
    72	(`S-F1`, `S-F4`). **The vector index** is for the same discovery when the question's wording differs from the
    73	entry's, once the documented miss trigger has fired (`V-F11`, `V-F13`, `M4`). **The code graph** is only for a
    74	multi-hop structural question about Python code, after its trigger, and only as a lower bound (`G-F8`, `G-F17`,
    75	`G-F18`, `M5`). Everything else is the files.
    76	
    77	| the question | store | if not, this | evidence |
    78	|---|---|---|---|
    79	| What do we already know about X? (free text, words likely in the entry) | **FTS5** — conditional on the size trigger | `INDEX.md`, grep, then read | S-F1, S-F4 |
    80	| Which prior entry matches when my words differ from its? (find by meaning) | **vector** — conditional on the miss trigger | `INDEX.md`, grep, then read | V-F11, V-F13, M4 |
    81	| A question about the code itself: what changing F reaches, which tests reach F | see **Part 2** (code and docs) | — | Part 2 |
    82	| An exact identifier, an error string, a known `file:line` | none | literal grep or filename lookup, then read | V-F7, V-F21, s3-F29 |
    83	| Every active entry covering path X — completeness | none — a ranked top-k cannot prove completeness | exhaustive scan of `INDEX.md` and entry headers | V-F1, S-F13, G-F14 |
    84	| Proving absence ("have we never recorded…") | none — no hit is not proof | exhaustive search, then read | V-F20 |
    85	| A structured header question (status, `supersedes`, `depends_on`) | none | `INDEX.md` scan or a script over frontmatter; follow links by grep | V-F22, M13 |
    86	| Why did we choose this, or why is this line like this? | none | the entry's **Why:** and its evidence; `git blame`, `git log -S` | M19, SOURCE_OF_TRUTH |
    87	| Is this decision still true of the code? | none | read the entry and the code before acting on it; a mismatch is reported to EJ | SOURCE_OF_TRUTH Rd5 |
    88	| What should I load when resuming? | none | `INDEX.md` and the task's `state.md` | M17, M18, M19 |
    89	| A map of the whole repo | none | `PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md`, `INDEX.md` | M18, G-F18 |
    90	| A `covers:` path that is not Python | none | git and grep | G-F18 |
    91	| Is the graph I am about to query current? | none — do not check, rebuild whole | rebuild, or use git | G-F5, G-F18 |
    92	
    93	**Costs, and where there are none.** Measured on other corpora or on this machine, never on a real
    94	`project-memory/`: grep over 10,000 synthetic entries, 0.038 s for one word and 0.060 s for a structured chain
    95	(`S-F13`); grep plus opening every hit, about 226K tokens per question (`S-F4`, `S-F13` — opencode's figure, and D3
    96	records that the other two report no such measurement; `S-F4` measures other corpora, not memory entries); an
    97	`INDEX.md` scan, under 0.01 s and minimal tokens
    98	(`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`); reading one entry, roughly 270 tokens by the 4-characters-per-token assumption (`S-F5`, not
    99	measured); a git last-change lookup at the tool's timing resolution (`G-F12`). **`GAP` for everything that matters
   100	most**: no FTS5-vs-grep measurement on short entries (`S-F5`), no vector measurement on a corpus shaped like
   101	`entries/` (`V-F11`), no graph-vs-grep head-to-head on this repo's own questions (`G-F10`, `M21`), and no wrapper
   102	exists to time a filtered graph query (`G-F18`). The graph-vs-grep head-to-head is now measured on this repo's own
   103	code and docs (BM-1–BM-9, below); the vector-on-`entries/` gap and a real `project-memory/` corpus are not. See
   104	**Measured here: development and testing** below.
   105	
   106	**Still unresolved.** Six disagreements the three engines did not settle, kept as positions and not averaged (D1–D6
   107	in the run record): whether FTS5 or the vector index comes first, or whether memory needs a store at all; whether the
   108	FTS/vector benefit has been measured at all; the true token cost of grep-plus-open; the cost of `INDEX.md`; whether
   109	`INDEX.md` carries `covers:` and status; and what counts as a *fired* trigger. **For code and docs, the measurements
   110	settle three of them** (my reading): D1, full-text before vector (BM-4, BM-5); D2, the full-text benefit is measured
   111	on docs (BM-4, BM-6); D3, grep printed about 4,470 characters to the right file on decision questions, not ~226K
   112	tokens (BM-4). For memory entries all six stay open.
   113	
   114	**Why no SQLite is visible in this repo.** Three reasons, and none is a rejection. (Since 2026-10-12 three SQLite
   115	caches do exist, in the measurement run's workspace: `runs/20261012-caches-routing/bench/.cache/` holds `fts.sqlite`,
   116	`vec.sqlite` and `graph.sqlite`, 9.5, 10.2 and 4.9 MB (BM-9). They are gitignored and rebuilt there, not part of the
   117	product.) The patterns are gitignored and
   118	the indexes are rebuilt on demand. The SQLite this repo does have, `ancient_games/index.py`,
   119	indexes the **referee's journal**, not memory: it is the working precedent for this exact pattern (7 tables, derived,
   120	~8 ms per journal) but it answers a different question. And the memory cache is gated behind a trigger that has not
   121	fired, because `project-memory/` does not exist yet. The same is true of the code graph: `code_graph/code_graph.py`
   122	already exists, deterministic and full-rebuild, and is the option this document names if that trigger fires.
   123	
   124	A fourth reason belongs to the first draft. Its research asked only whether each cache is needed to search memory
   125	*entries*, so it measured nothing on the questions asked during development and testing. The section **Measured here:
   126	development and testing** measures those.
   127	
   128	### Part 2 — code and docs (corpus: this repo's code and docs)
   129	
   130	**Outside the vision.** The confirmed vision covers memory entries only (EJ, 2026-10-12). This table is measured on
   131	this repo's code and docs and is kept as evidence and as guidance for agents; it is not part of the caches design.
   132	Its evidence is **Measured here: development and testing** below.
   133	
   134	**Routing rule for development and testing (proposed).**
   135	
   136	| the question | use | then | evidence |
   137	|---|---|---|---|
   138	| Where is function or method X defined? | code graph | read that definition | BM-1 |
   139	| Where is a flag, constant or string defined? | full-text, or grep | read the hit | BM-1 (A05) |
   140	| What calls X? | code graph — lower bound, Python only | read the callers; treat the list as partial | BM-3 (C08–C10: 33–46 characters) |
   141	| What does changing F reach, several hops out? | code graph — Python only, lower bound | grep imports and callers for the rest, then read | G-F8, G-F17, G-F18 (not measured here) |
   142	| Which tests should I run after changing function X or file F? | code graph (tests reaching X or F) **plus** full-text on the file name (tests that run it as a separate process) | run that subset; run the full suite (31–33 s) before committing | BM-7, BM-8, BM-9 |
   143	| Why did we decide X? Where is X explained in the docs? | full-text (ties hybrid on hits; prints less in total) | read the section | BM-4 |
   144	| How do I run or configure X? | not settled: hybrid and vector scored 0.5 and full-text 0.33, on 6 questions | read the hit | BM-5 (n=6, too small to act on) |
   145	| A concept, in my own words | grep and read, as an agent search | record a miss if the answer is found another way | BM-2 |
   146	| Anything the graph answers with nothing or only partly | full-text, then grep | — | BM-8 |
   147	
   148	This table is for the **code and docs**. Part 1 above is for **memory entries**; it is reasoned, not measured, and
   149	three of its citations were corrected in this merge. The two
   150	differ because the corpus differs. Code and docs exist today and are already large enough that full-text and the
   151	graph save reading (BM-6). `project-memory/` does not exist yet.
   152	
   153	**What this does not change, and what it proposes.**
   154	- **Unchanged:** the rules for all three caches below (full rebuild, rebuild when stale, fall back when absent).
   155	  B1–Rd4 (Core requirements) are the acceptance criteria for the memory caches.
   156	- **The vision stands as confirmed:** for code and docs, the measurements say full-text and the graph already save
   157	  reading today. EJ kept the vision for memory entries only (2026-10-12), so the proposals in
   158	  `runs/20261012-caches-routing/vision-proposals.md` (P1–P3) were not adopted.
   159	
   160	## Rules for all three caches
   161	
   162	These apply to whichever cache is built. They come from how the researched tools fail.
   163	
   164	- **Full rebuild, never patched.** A live, incrementally updated index drifted silently from a clean rebuild: 4.3% of
   165	  edges wrong, with every integrity check passing (`G-F5 drift`). Its makers decided drift cannot be measured cheaply
   166	  (`G-F5c`). A full rebuild of this repo's code graph takes 0.94 s (`G-F12`); the benchmark's own code-graph rebuild
   167	  takes 0.89 s (BM-9); an FTS rebuild of 10,000 synthetic entries takes 0.53 s (`S-F13`).
   168	- **Rebuild before answering when stale.** Before each query, compare the recorded content hash of the
   169	  sources with the index and rebuild if they differ. Build into a temporary file and rename it over the old one
   170	  (`S-F9`, quoted: *"A stale index is worse than no index."*).
   171	- **Fall back when absent.** If the cache file or SQLite's FTS5 is missing, the tool answers with grep and says so in
   172	  every answer (Rd3.2) (`S-F9`); the same for a missing embedding model (my reading, extending `S-F9`). A mistyped
   173	  database path silently creates an empty database, which looks exactly like a stale one (`G-F5 drift`, failure
   174	  mode 5), so the tool opens the cache read-only and treats "no such file" as absent.
   175	- **Index only active entries** — for `project-memory/` — the same set as `INDEX.md`, with `status` and `covers`
   176	  stored beside each row (`V-F22`, my reading). Otherwise superseded entries come back looking current.
   177	
   178	## SQLite full-text index
   179	
   180	### Five whys
   181	
   182	1. **Why a full-text index?** To find entries by their words, ranked, with a snippet, instead of grep's unranked list
   183	   (`S-F1`, quoted: *"grep returns unranked matches."*).
   184	2. **Why does ranking matter?** Every hit the agent opens costs context. In the one controlled study, BM25 used 5.8K
   185	   tokens per question against 226K for an agent using grep over files, on its smallest corpus: 1,144 documents,
   186	   1.7M tokens (`S-F4`).
   187	3. **Why not build it now?** That study puts the point where BM25 overtakes the grep agent at around 10 million tokens
   188	   of text, and the grep agent led at the smallest sizes (`S-F4`). At our entry size that is about 37,000 entries
   189	   (`S-F5`, my reading via its 4-characters-per-token assumption). `INDEX.md` already lists every active entry. In the
   190	   researcher's own test, at 10,000 synthetic entries a one-word grep took 0.038 s and a grep chain for a header
   191	   question 0.06 s; at 50,000 the header chain took 0.15 s, and the one-word timing was invalid (`S-F13`). No study
   192	   covers a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries (`S-F5`: not measured).
   193	4. **Why might it be needed later?** `INDEX.md` stops being enough once it can no longer list every active entry under
   194	   its size limit. The agent then loses the one-line scan of every entry, and ranked search is what replaces it (my
   195	   reading).
   196	5. **Why only as a cache?** The markdown is the source of truth, and the cache must be rebuildable and skippable (M6).
   197	
   198	Structured questions such as "every active decision covering path X" or "what supersedes Y" do not need SQLite. A
   199	short script reading the entry headers answers them in 0.1 s at 10,000 entries (`S-F13`). That measures time, not
   200	tokens; my reading is that the script and an SQL query return the same list of entries, so the agent reads the same
   201	amount either way (not measured).
   202	
   203	### 5W1H
   204	
   205	| | |
   206	|---|---|
   207	| **Who** | Built and rebuilt by a script with no model call. Read by any agent on demand. EJ decides to build it when the trigger fires. |
   208	| **What** | One FTS5 table with `kind`, `status` and `covers` as `UNINDEXED` columns (`S-F2`). One row per active entry file: the research leaves the row grain open (`S-F17`); my reading is that an entry of one or two sentences is already one section. The default `unicode61` tokenizer: in the researcher's own one-row test, a plausible change to keep paths whole made ordinary queries return zero rows silently, and ids such as `D-0012` had to be quoted (`S-F16`). |
   209	| **When** | Built when the trigger fires: `INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry. Rebuilt before any query that finds it stale. Never read at session start. |
   210	| **Where** | `project-memory/.cache/index.sqlite`, gitignored. |
   211	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   212	| **How** | One stdlib `sqlite3` script, the smallest option in the SQLite node (§5, option B): rebuild to a temporary file and rename; compare the recorded content hash for staleness, so a deleted or uncommitted change is caught (K1.1, K2.1); fall back to `rg`. Nothing to install: SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5 works here (verified 2026-10-12). |
   213	
   214	## Vector index
   215	
   216	### Five whys
   217	
   218	1. **Why a vector index?** To find an entry by meaning when the question shares no words with it.
   219	2. **Why can't grep or full-text search do that?** They match words. But the evidence does not show vectors winning on
   220	   memory text. (They do win on some code tasks, `V-F5`, and on half the file-based pairs in `V-F3`.) On conversational
   221	   memory, grep returned inline beat vector search for every harness and model pair (`V-F3`). On a 500,000-document
   222	   corpus, BM25 beat vectors even on the questions built to favour them (`V-F4`). On decision-style records, a
   223	   structured graph and a vector memory tool were "largely equivalent" on relevance, and plain notes search did no
   224	   better on listing or supersession questions (s3-F34; `V-F1`).
   225	3. **Why not build it now?** No measurement exists on a corpus shaped like `entries/` (`V-F11`: not measured).
   226	   Anthropic's 2024 guidance is that under about 200,000 tokens a knowledge base can simply be put in the prompt
   227	   (`V-F8`), roughly 400–650 entries (`V-F12`, my reading). This design does not do that (it never loads the whole
   228	   tree), so this is only a bound: below it, retrieval quality is not what limits an agent (my reading). Vectors also
   229	   add failures of their own: negation (`V-F20`), exact ids and paths (`V-F21`), and a second rebuild step that can lag
   230	   the first (`V-F24`).
   231	4. **Why might it be needed later?** If agents keep missing entries that exist because their words differ from the
   232	   entry's. The trigger is a record of such misses: the entry existed, `INDEX.md`, grep and full-text search all missed
   233	   it, and it was found another way (`V-F13`, my reading). The vector index is worth building when, on those recorded
   234	   misses, the hybrid index finds entries full-text did not, and it does no worse on the other questions (B1.2).
   235	5. **Why hybrid only?** On memory-style text, the positive results found combine vectors with keyword search (`V-F6`,
   236	   `V-F7`; `V-F10` is unproven), and vectors alone were the weakest arm when results were returned inline (`V-F3`,
   237	   `V-F4`). So the vector index comes after the full-text index, never
   238	   instead of it. This is a **proposed** policy: on half the file-based pairs in `V-F3` vectors won alone (why 2
   239	   above), so the evidence does not compel it.
   240	
   241	### 5W1H
   242	
   243	| | |
   244	|---|---|
   245	| **Who** | Built and rebuilt by a script with no model call except the embedding model itself. Read by any agent on demand, through the same command as full-text search. EJ decides to build it when the trigger fires. |
   246	| **What** | One vector per active entry file, with the entry's `status`, `covers`, content hash and embedding-model id stored beside it (`V-F24`: a changed model makes every vector useless). Results merged with the full-text results by reciprocal rank fusion (vector node §Q5, from qmd's README). |
   247	| **When** | After the full-text index exists, and when the B1.2 comparison favours hybrid. Rebuilt with the full-text index, in the same step, so the two cannot disagree (`V-F24`, my reading). |
   248	| **Where** | `project-memory/.cache/`, beside `index.sqlite`, gitignored. |
   249	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   250	| **How** | The smallest options in `V-F18`/`V-F19`: a static Model2Vec model with numpy (option A, no GPU) or `bge-small-en-v1.5` through FastEmbed (option B). Both are small next to the 16 GB GPU here. Embedding speed on this GPU is **not measured** (`V-F18`). No embedding runtime is installed today; numpy is (`V-F19`). |
   251	
   252	## Code graph
   253	
   254	### Five whys
   255	
   256	1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code, such as what an edit touches.
   257	2. **Why would memory need that?** For "which entries cover the files I am editing" (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd4.1). The
   258	   drift check it once also served was removed by EJ on 2026-10-12.
   259	3. **Why is a text search enough?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
   260	   to path (`G-F14`), so finding the entries that cover a file is a search over `covers:` lines.
   261	4. **Why not add a graph anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that a text search does not have
   262	   (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but it
   263	   covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
   264	   Whether an entry is still true is answered by reading it against the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd5), not by a
   265	   graph.
   266	5. **Why might it be needed later?** Either of two conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:
   267	   - `covers:` starts naming functions or classes instead of files;
   268	   - an agent must answer about files not on any `covers:` list ("what does this change affect").
   269	
   270	### 5W1H
   271	
   272	| | |
   273	|---|---|
   274	| **Who** | Any agent, by a text search over `covers:` lines. EJ decides to build a graph if one of the two conditions holds. |
   275	| **What** | Today: no graph for memory. A text search over `covers:` lines. If a condition holds: the repo's own `code_graph/code_graph.py` (Python only, deterministic, full rebuild, no incremental mode, `G-F18`) before any outside tool. |
   276	| **When** | Today: before editing a file (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd4.1). A graph, if ever built, is rebuilt in full each time it is used. |
   277	| **Where** | Nothing today. Later, `project-memory/.cache/code-graph.json`, gitignored. |
   278	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   279	| **How** | `rg` for the file's path in the `covers:` lines of `entries/`. External indexers (codegraph) are not adopted: their win is measured on source-code architecture questions, not on memory (`G-F7`, `G-F14`), and they bring the drift in `G-F5 drift`. |
   280	
   281	## Measured here: development and testing
   282	
   283	EJ's aim is that development and testing get faster and cheaper in tokens. So this section measures all three stores
   284	against grep on **this repo**: 490 files (123 `.py`, 367 `.md`, 4.8 MB), with 40 questions an agent asks while
   285	developing and testing it. Run: `runs/20261012-caches-routing/` (results in `bench/results.md`, ids `BM-*` below).
   286	
   287	**Who wrote each check, and what it does not establish.**
   288	- The **questions and answers** were written by codex (`gpt-6.1-sol`), each checked by an `rg` command it ran. Five
   289	  Claude agents re-checked all 40: 37 were correct and 3 were incomplete. Those 3 were completed.
   290	- **"Which tests run this code"** is also scored against per-test runtime coverage (`pytest-cov`, no model). That
   291	  counts in-process Python only, so it misses tests that run the code as a separate process.
   292	- The **harness** was written by opencode. A Claude audit found 5 high-severity biases, and a Claude repair fixed
   293	  them; the repair was **not re-audited**. Rebuilding from scratch gave the same answers on all 180 question-route
   294	  pairs.
   295	- **Token cost** is measured as *characters printed up to the first line that names a right file*. That is a proxy,
   296	  not a token count, and a weak one:
   297	  - it stops at the right *file*, not the right answer;
   298	  - misses are left out, so each route's mean is over a different set of questions;
   299	  - each route prints a different format.
   300	
   301	  No agent was run end to end.
   302	- One repo, one question set, **n = 6 to 10 per category**. None of this is a project-memory corpus.
   303	
   304	**What each route returns, per category** (symbol hit in the top 5 / characters read to the first line that names a
   305	right file):
   306	
   307	| | grep (`rg`) | SQLite full-text | vector | hybrid | code graph |
   308	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
   309	| **BM-1** exact identifier (8) | 0.75 / 920 | 0.63 / 356 | 0.0 / 837 | 0.5 / 91 | **0.75 / 43** |
   310	| **BM-2** concept in other words (8) | 0 / — | 0 / — | 0 (file 0.25) / 955 | 0 (file 0.13) / 1,427 | n/a |
   311	| **BM-3** structure and test impact (10) | 0.8 / 1,015 | **0.9 / 175** | 0.1 / 1,421 | 0.9 / 362 | 0.7 / **61** |
   312	| **BM-4** decision or reason in the docs (8) | 0.63 / 4,470 | **0.63 / 525** | 0.5 / 810 | 0.63 / 516 | 0.0 (over 2 questions) |
   313	| **BM-5** how to run or configure (6) | 0.17 / 6,875 | 0.33 / 1,146 | 0.5 / 569 | 0.5 / 1,120 | n/a |
   314	
   315	- **BM-6, all categories.** Characters printed up to the first right file (each mean over that route's hits only:
   316	  grep 26, full-text 25, vector 19, hybrid 26, graph 13): grep 2,688, full-text 451, hybrid 528, vector
   317	  923, graph 53 (graph over the 20 questions that name a symbol or file). Total output per answer: grep 4,304,
   318	  full-text 1,817.
   319	- **BM-7, test impact against runtime coverage** (C01–C04, n=4). The macro-average test-file recall of the tests that
   320	  really run the function each route found: graph 0.78; grep, full-text and hybrid 0.59; vector 0.04.
   321	- **BM-8, tests that run the code as a separate process** (C05–C07). The graph returns **nothing**, because no
   322	  call edge exists. Full-text search on the file name found 2 of 3; grep named a gold file on 2 of 3 and the
   323	  complete set on 1 of 3, printing up to 8.7K
   324	  characters.
   325	- **BM-9, speed.** Every route answers in at most 15 ms per question; the slowest was grep on E04, at 14.7 ms. Full
   326	  rebuilds take 0.17 s for full-text (9.5 MB), 0.94 s for vector (10.2 MB) and 0.89 s for the graph (4.9 MB); the
   327	  graph's own rebuild for `G-F12` was measured separately at 0.94 s. The full test suite, 529 tests, takes 31–33 s (3
   328	  runs).
   329	
   330	**What the numbers say (my reading).**
   331	1. **On this repo, speed is not the reason to use a cache.** Every route takes milliseconds (BM-9). The difference is
   332	   in **what the route prints**: full-text prints about a sixth of grep's characters before naming a right file
   333	   (BM-6). Whether that becomes a sixth of an agent's tokens is not measured. Where
   334	   the question names a symbol or file, the graph gets there in tens of characters (BM-1, BM-3); its BM-6 mean is over
   335	   those 20 questions only, so it is not a like-for-like ratio.
   336	2. **The vector index loses almost everywhere it was tried.** It never found an exact identifier (BM-1), and its
   337	   test-file recall against runtime coverage was 0.04 (BM-7). Its only edge is "how do I run or configure X" (BM-5,
   338	   n=6), which is
   339	   too small to act on. Nothing here contradicts the earlier rule that vectors come last and only as a hybrid.
   340	3. **No route answers a concept asked in other words in one shot** (BM-2). That still takes an agent searching and
   341	   reading. It is exactly the miss the vector trigger is meant to record.
   342	4. **No single store answers "which tests should I run".** The graph finds tests that reach the code indirectly
   343	   (BM-7), but is blind to tests that run it as a separate process (BM-8). Full-text search on the file name finds
   344	   the latter. An empty graph answer means *not seen*, never *no tests*. Even combined, the two routes
   345	   can miss affected tests: both missed C05 entirely, and the graph found only 0.33 and 0.8 of the runtime tests for
   346	   C01 and C02 (BM-7). That is why the full suite runs before committing.
   347	
   348	## Core requirements
   349	
   350	They serve the vision, `vision-statements/caches-memory-vision-statements.md`, and cover the two memory caches,
   351	full-text and vector; the code graph is its own project (`docs/code-graph/`). They are grouped by what happens to a
   352	cache: it is **built**, it is **kept** true, and it is **read** (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"let rewrite using these 3
   353	categories first"*). Each requirement names its source: **(v)** is the vision, **(d)** is this document's text, and
   354	*proposed* marks wording EJ has not decided. Each has a check, **Fails if**, that some case breaks. There are no fixed
   355	numbers: where a limit applies, it is a comparison that measurement decides. The ids R1–R9 they replace are given in
   356	brackets, because run records cite them.
   357	
   358	### Build — when a cache comes to exist, and what goes in it
   359	
   360	**B1 — Nothing is built before its trigger fires** (v: never built before its own trigger; was R5)
   361	Each cache has an observable trigger. This is the requirement that keeps the whole layer honest. The query-time
   362	counterpart — which store to reach for once a trigger has fired — is the section **When to use which store** above.
   363	
   364	- **B1.0 EJ decides** — when a cache's comparison passes, EJ decides whether it is built; a passed comparison alone
   365	  builds nothing (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"EJ decides"*; the 5W1H tables below say the same).
   366	- **B1.1 Full-text** — built only when, on the project's own recorded questions (Rd4), full-text reaches the right
   367	  entry after reading less than `INDEX.md` plus grep does, with no more misses. Measured as the code-and-docs benchmark
   368	  was (BM-1 to BM-9): the same questions through both routes, the characters read to the first right entry, and the
   369	  misses. Characters read is a proxy, not a token count, and a weak one: it stops at the right file, not the right
   370	  answer (**Measured here**, who wrote each check). The comparison decides; no fixed size is set in advance (S-F5,
   371	  S-F13: nothing is measured yet for a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries). (was R5.1)
   372	  - **The comparison** runs on a trial index built in `runs/<run-id>/`, never in `project-memory/.cache/`
   373	    (*proposed*). It is rerun when an agent records a miss, and when EJ asks; the agent that runs it writes the result
   374	    there; if it passes, the agent tells EJ, who decides (B1.0) (*proposed*, the same as B1.2; EJ, 2026-10-12:
   375	    *"give B1.1 the same rerun wording"*).
   376	- **B1.2 Vector** — built only when, on the recorded questions and misses (Rd4), the vector index finds an entry that
   377	  full-text missed, and loses nothing full-text already found. (was R5.2; EJ, 2026-10-12: *"ok write it into
   378	  CACHES.md"*)
   379	  - **A miss** is a question where the entry existed, `INDEX.md`, grep and full-text search all missed it, and the
   380	    agent found it another way (V-F13). The agent that found it records it (Rd4).
   381	  - **Finds** means the right entry is in the top 5 results (*proposed*; the benchmark's measure, BM-1 to BM-5).
   382	  - **Loses nothing** means: every recorded question full-text answers is still answered, and the characters read to
   383	    the first right entry are no more than full-text's (*proposed*; the same measure as B1.1, with its weakness).
   384	  - **The comparison** runs on a trial index built in `runs/<run-id>/`, never in `project-memory/.cache/`
   385	    (*proposed*). It is rerun when an agent records a miss, and when EJ asks; the agent that runs it writes the result
   386	    there; if it passes, the agent tells EJ, who decides (B1.0) (*proposed*).
   387	  - *Proposed* (hybrid only, **Vector index** why 5; EJ, 2026-10-12: *"label hybrid as proposed"*): the vector index
   388	    is combined with full-text, so it is built only after B1.1 has fired, and the comparison runs on the hybrid
   389	    index.
   390	  - **Fails if** a vector index exists in `project-memory/.cache/` with no recorded comparison in which it found at
   391	    least one missed entry and lost none of full-text's answers, or with no decision by EJ.
   392	- **Fails if** a cache exists under `project-memory/.cache/` with no recorded comparison showing its trigger fired,
   393	  or with no decision by EJ to build it.
   394	
   395	**B2 — Derived from the files** (v: never the source of truth; was R1)
   396	The cache is computed from the memory's markdown and never holds knowledge of its own (M4, M6).
   397	
   398	- **B2.1** Deleting the cache and rebuilding from the files restores every answer, provided the vector index is
   399	  rebuilt with the same embedding model (a changed model makes every vector useless, V-F24). *Example:* `rm -rf
   400	  project-memory/.cache/` → run the documented rebuild → the same query returns the same entries. (M6) (was R1.1)
   401	- **B2.2** The cache directory is ignored by git and a rebuild leaves `git status --short` clean. (M6) (was R1.3)
   402	- **Fails if** the same query returns different entries after a delete and a rebuild with the same embedding model,
   403	  or a rebuild leaves `git status --short` dirty.
   404	
   405	**B3 — Full rebuild, never patched** (v: never patched; was R2.1)
   406	- **B3.1** The documented rebuild regenerates the whole index from the files; no incremental update path exists.
   407	  *Example:* the rebuild command has no "update" mode. (G-F5 drift)
   408	- **Fails if** the rebuild has an update or incremental mode.
   409	
   410	**B4 — Only active entries, with their status** (d: Rules for all three caches, my reading; was R6)
   411	- **B4.1** The index holds the same set of entries as `INDEX.md`, each row carrying `status` and `covers`.
   412	  *Example:* a superseded entry never appears in a search result as if current. (V-F22, my reading)
   413	- **Fails if** the index holds an entry `INDEX.md` does not list as active, lacks one that `INDEX.md` does list, or
   414	  has a row without `status` or `covers`.
   415	
   416	**B5 — No model call to rebuild, except embedding** (v: How we know it works; was R8.1)
   417	- **B5.1** The documented rebuild completes without a model call, except the embedding step of B1.2.
   418	  *Example:* rebuild with no model credentials available; the full-text path still works. (M4, M6)
   419	- **Fails if** the full-text rebuild calls a model, or the vector rebuild calls any model but its embedding model.
   420	
   421	### Keep — the cache stays true to the files, and stays worth having
   422	
   423	**K1 — It records what it was built from** (d: Rules for all three caches; was R2.2)
   424	- **K1.1** The index records a content hash of the entry files it was built from, so a stale one is detectable,
   425	  including edits not yet committed (*proposed*: a commit alone misses those). A newest-mtime check is not enough:
   426	  a deletion leaves no newer file (K2.1). (S-F9)
   427	- **K1.2** The vector index also records the id of the embedding model it was built with, because a changed model
   428	  makes every vector useless (V-F24; the vector 5W1H stores it beside each vector).
   429	- **Fails if** the index carries no content hash of its sources, or the vector index carries no embedding-model id.
   430	
   431	**K2 — Stale never answers** (d: Rules for all three caches; was R3.1)
   432	- **K2.1** Before answering, the tool compares the recorded content hash with the entry files, and for the vector
   433	  index the recorded model id with the current one (*proposed*). If either differs it rebuilds, building into a
   434	  temporary file and renaming over the old one — so a deleted entry cannot stay searchable. (S-F9)
   435	- **Fails if** after an entry is added, changed or deleted, or the embedding model changes, a query answers from the
   436	  old index.
   437	
   438	**K3 — Dropped when it stops paying** (d; vision: *"cheap … and not before"*; was R8.2; EJ, 2026-10-12: *"yes please
   439	write"*)
   440	- **K3.1** A built cache keeps passing its own trigger: its B1 comparison is rerun on the recorded questions (Rd4),
   441	  and a query's cost includes any rebuild it triggered (K2) (*proposed*).
   442	- **K3.2** The comparison is rerun when an agent records a miss, and when EJ asks; the agent that runs it writes the
   443	  result to `runs/<run-id>/` (*proposed*, the same as B1.2).
   444	- **K3.3** If the cache no longer passes, the agent tells EJ, who decides whether to drop it. Dropping is deleting it;
   445	  memory is unaffected (B2, Rd3) (*proposed*, mirroring B1.0).
   446	- **Fails if** a cache stays in `project-memory/.cache/` after a rerun it failed, with no decision by EJ to keep it.
   447	
   448	### Read — an agent queries it
   449	
   450	**Rd1 — Never read at session start** (v: never read at session start; was R9)
   451	- **Rd1.1** No cache file is part of what a session loads at start. *Example:* the session-start reads in
   452	  `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` (§ When it will be used) name no file under `project-memory/.cache/`; a start hook that
   453	  opens `.cache/index.sqlite` fails this. (M18)
   454	- **Fails if** a start hook or instruction opens any file under `project-memory/.cache/`.
   455	
   456	**Rd2 — An answer names its entry** (d; was R7)
   457	- **Rd2.1** Every result prints the entry file it came from, so the agent can open the source before acting on it
   458	  (M19: *"open the primary source when a retrieved statement will determine an action"*). *Example:* search output
   459	  names `entries/D-0012-*.md`. (*proposed*: "the agent", not "a human", per M19 and the vision; EJ, 2026-10-12:
   460	  *"write fixes 1–4 into CACHES.md"*)
   461	- **Fails if** a result does not name its entry file.
   462	
   463	**Rd3 — Absence degrades, it does not fail** (v: delete the folder and memory is unaffected; was R1.2, R3.2, R4)
   464	- **Rd3.1** Memory is fully readable with no cache present. *Example:* with `.cache/` absent, session start and an
   465	  on-demand `grep` over `entries/` both work. (M6) (was R1.2)
   466	- **Rd3.2** A missing cache file or a missing FTS5 falls back to grep, and every fallback answer says it came from
   467	  grep, not the cache, so the agent knows its results are unranked. (S-F9) (was R4.1; *proposed*: "says so in every
   468	  answer" replaces "warns once")
   469	- **Rd3.3** A missing embedding model does the same. *(my reading, extending S-F9.)* (was R4.2)
   470	- **Rd3.4** A wrong path is never mistaken for an empty index: the cache is opened read-only and "no such file" is
   471	  treated as absent. *Example:* point the tool at a typo'd path → it falls back to grep and says so, it does not
   472	  create an empty database. (G-F5, failure mode 5) (was R3.2)
   473	- **Rd3.5** A rebuild that fails falls back to grep; it never answers from the stale index (K2) (*proposed*).
   474	- **Fails if** with the cache, FTS5 or the embedding model missing, the path mistyped, or a rebuild failing, a query
   475	  errors, answers from an empty or stale index, or answers from grep without saying so.
   476	
   477	**Rd4 — Questions and misses are recorded** (*proposed*; new)
   478	- **Rd4.1** The questions agents ask of the memory are recorded, and so is each miss: an entry existed, `INDEX.md`,
   479	  grep and full-text search all missed it, and it was found another way (the definition in B1.2). B1 and K3 run on
   480	  this record.
   481	- **Rd4.2** The agent that asked the question records it, as it happens (*proposed*).
   482	- **Rd4.3** The record lives in `project-memory/questions/`, one file per agent session, committed to git
   483	  (*proposed*). Not in `.cache/`: the record is a source, and deleting the cache must not delete it. One file per
   484	  session, for the reason `journal/` has one (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` W6.1): agents never append to one shared file. Not
   485	  inside `journal/`: the journal is raw notes that become entries, while this record is read by the comparison.
   486	- **Rd4.4** One record holds: the question as asked; the routes tried, in order (`INDEX.md`, grep, full-text,
   487	  vector); the entry that answered it, or "not found"; whether it was a miss, and if so how the entry was found; the
   488	  date; and the agent's model id (*proposed*).
   489	- **Not yet done:** `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` § Where it is put does not list `questions/`; adding it waits for EJ.
   490	- **Fails if** `project-memory/` has entries and B1 or K3 has no recorded questions to run on, or a record lacks a
   491	  field of Rd4.4.
   492	
   493	## Example numbers by case
   494	
   495	The triggers in B1 and K3 are comparisons. The measurement decides them, so no number is fixed in advance. This
   496	section shows **what those comparisons have come out at elsewhere**, by agent type and condition, so a reader knows
   497	roughly where a project stands before measuring. These are examples with sources, not thresholds.
   498	
   499	Run: `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/`. Its source ids are:
   500	- `W-*`: web research (`nodes/web.md`);
   501	- `OC`: opencode's research (`nodes/opencode.md`);
   502	- `SZ`: the SEZA measurement (`nodes/seza.md`; every number with its command);
   503	- `CX`: codex's independent second look at SEZA, by agent type (`nodes/codex.md`);
   504	- `BM-*`, `S-F*`, `V-F*`, `G-F*`, `s3-F*`: as above.
   505	
   506	Web quotes are model-made extracts, not byte-checked, except W-1. Numbers marked *my arithmetic* are computed from a
   507	sourced figure.
   508	
   509	### The index read at session start (`INDEX.md`, `MEMORY.md`), by agent type
   510	
   511	| Agent | Window | Limit on files loaded at start | Measured effective context | Example |
   512	|---|---|---|---|---|
   513	| Claude Code (Claude 5.x) | 1M (W-8) | `MEMORY.md`: "first 200 lines … or the first 25KB, whichever comes first"; `CLAUDE.md`: target under 200 lines (W-1) | Opus 4.6, MRCR v2: 76% at 1M (W-14); 93% at 256K (W-15, reported) | 25 KB binds first: about 150 lines at 167 B per line (W-1), about 107 lines at SEZA's 233 B per line (*my arithmetic*, SZ) |
   514	| Codex (GPT-6.x) | 1.05M per the API (W-9), but this machine's Codex model cache says `context_window: 500000`, 95% effective, so about 475K (CX) | `AGENTS.md` combined up to 32 KiB (`project_doc_max_bytes`) (W-2) | GPT-5.4: 79.3% at 128K–256K, 36.6% at 512K–1M (W-15, reported) | instructions plus index under 32 KiB |
   515	| agy (Gemini 3.1 Pro / 3.8 Flash) | 1,048,576 (W-9, CX) | 24,000 bytes per rule file, **and** a 20,000-token budget across all rules (CX, official page); 12,000 characters in other reports (W-4, conflict) | 84.9% at 128K, 26.3% at 1M (W-15, reported) | under 12,000 characters if both readings must hold (*my reading*) |
   516	| opencode (MiniMax M3 / M2.7) | 1M / 204,800 (W-9) | no limit stated (W-6) | GAP | follow the strictest agent sharing the repo |
   517	| dsh (DeepSeek flash / V4 pro) | 1,048,576 (CX) | a 65,536-byte budget for the instruction files it loads (CX, from its loader docs) | GAP | instructions plus index under 64 KiB |
   518	| Qwen Code | — | its auto-memory index is capped at 200 lines or 25,000 bytes (`MAX_MANAGED_AUTO_MEMORY_INDEX_LINES` / `_BYTES`, CX); it also warns when always-loaded context passes 15% of the window, an advisory only (CX) | — | the index cap binds first: under 25,000 bytes, as for Claude |
   519	| Cursor | — | rules "under 500 lines" (W-3) | — | — |
   520	
   521	**Why the start load should stay small, whatever the window:**
   522	- Half of 17 models kept satisfactory performance only up to 32K (RULER, W-10).
   523	- 11 models fell below 50% of their baseline at 32K when the question and the answer share few words (NoLiMa, W-11).
   524	- Focused prompts of about 300 tokens beat full ones of about 113K (W-13).
   525	- Context files raised inference cost by over 20%, with no general success gain (W-17).
   526	- At 500 simultaneous instructions, the best model was 68% accurate (W-16).
   527	
   528	**Use case, SEZA (SZ, CX):**
   529	- The start load by agent, as estimates: Claude Code 61,993 B (about 15.4K tokens, including the global `CLAUDE.md`;
   530	  SZ measured 58,427 B without it); dsh 46,319 B (about 11.5K); Codex and agy 29,166 B each (about 7.2K) (CX). All are
   531	  well under 15% of every window above.
   532	- `MEMORY.md` is 52 lines and 12,108 B, within Claude's limit.
   533	- The 52 topic files (159,524 B) would not fit in 25 KB. That is why they are an index plus files read on demand.
   534	
   535	**When several agents share a project, the binding limit is the smallest one among them** (*my reading*).
   536	
   537	### B1.1: full-text against `INDEX.md` plus grep
   538	
   539	| Case | What was measured | Source |
   540	|---|---|---|
   541	| 1,144 docs, 1.7M tokens | the grep agent is more accurate (77.4 vs 74.7) but reads 226K tokens per question against BM25's 5.8K | W-22 |
   542	| 6,980 docs, 8.5M tokens | accuracy crossover: BM25 70.1 vs 69.9; BM25 ahead at every larger tier | W-22, S-F4 |
   543	| 511,959 docs, 600.8M tokens | BM25 50.5 vs the grep agent 30.7 | W-22 |
   544	| This repo's code and docs, 490 files, 4.8 MB | full-text prints about a sixth of grep's characters before naming a right file | BM-6 |
   545	| SEZA memory: the Claude memory folder (55 files) and a wider set of 257 files (2.5 MB) | grep takes 4–7 ms but prints from 361 B (a narrow term, memory folder) to 109,519 B, about 27K tokens (a broad term, 257-file set). Over the 257 files, a full-text proxy ranked the right file first on 3 of 5 questions and missed on 2 (n=5, one reader) | SZ |
   546	| A tool result too large to read | filtering in code instead of reading: 150,000 tokens down to 2,000 | OC (Anthropic, 2025-11-04) |
   547	
   548	**My reading:** on accuracy, grep stays competitive until millions of tokens. On **tokens read**, full-text wins much
   549	earlier: on SEZA-sized memory, a single broad term already floods grep's output. The B1.1 comparison measures exactly
   550	that.
   551	
   552	### B1.2: the vector index
   553	
   554	| Case | What was measured | Source |
   555	|---|---|---|
   556	| Loading about 115K tokens of history instead of retrieving | 30%–55% lower accuracy (GPT-4o, Llama 3.1) | W-18 |
   557	| Long context against retrieval, 100K–1M tokens | retrieval better for 3 of 4 models at 100K, and for all 4 from 500K | W-19 |
   558	| Vendor guidance | under about 200,000 tokens, the whole base can go in the prompt | V-F8 (2024) |
   559	| Dense against BM25, 1,144–511,959 docs | dense lowest at every tier in the table (7 tiers) | W-22 |
   560	| Enterprise corpus, 500K docs | vector 51.4 vs BM25 68.8 correctness | V-F4 |
   561	| Code, 1,000 or more files | +12.5% accuracy with vector search (Cursor), but 15 days later Cursor shipped Instant Grep and says it "does not store embeddings of your codebase for search" | V-F6, OC |
   562	| A memory growing 10× (1M to 10M tokens) | Mem0 64.1 to 48.6 (vendor's figures) | W-20 |
   563	| SEZA | no recorded misses, so the comparison has nothing to run on | SZ |
   564	
   565	**My reading:** retrieval beats loading everything somewhere around 100K–500K tokens, but it is keyword retrieval
   566	that wins. Vectors earn a place only on recorded meaning-misses, which is what B1.2 tests.
   567	
   568	### K3: rebuild cost
   569	
   570	| Store | Measured | Source |
   571	|---|---|---|
   572	| Full-text | 0.169 s for 490 files (index 9.5 MB, about 2× the source); 0.53 s for 10,000 entries; 2.57 s for 50,000; SEZA 0.004 s for 55 files, 0.54 s for 2,013 md files (34 MB), both in an in-memory database, so no disk writes (SZ) | BM-9, S-F13, SZ |
   573	| Vector | 0.936 s for 490 files with model2vec; embedding speed 81,018 samples/s for model2vec against 274 for a MiniLM fine-tune (model2vec's own table); sqlite-vec builds 1M × 128 dims in about 4 s, embedding excluded | BM-9, OC, W-27 |
   574	
   575	**My reading:** at the sizes of this repo (490 files) and of SEZA's whole markdown corpus (2,013 files; its memory
   576	stores alone are 152–201 files), a rebuild takes under a second (SEZA's figure is in-memory, not on disk). Larger
   577	corpora take seconds (2.57 s for 50,000 entries; about 4 s for sqlite-vec at 1M vectors, embedding
   578	excluded). A heavy embedding model adds the most per file.
   579	So K3 starts to bite with corpus size and with a slow embedding model.
   580	
   581	### SEZA today, trigger by trigger (SZ, the researcher's reading)
   582	- **B1.1:** no verdict at n=5.
   583	- **B1.2:** not met.
   584	- **K3:** cheap.
   585	
   586	SEZA's own `project_memory search` was used 42 times in 15 sessions, with none after 2026-09-24.
   587	
   588	**What actually went wrong in SEZA was not search** (CX):
   589	- Its start index carried outdated state: the cleanup audit records *"Memory index stale"*
   590	  (`seza/runs/20261010-project-cleanup/STATUS-GRAPH.md:268`).
   591	- Its derived graph was missing when it was asked for: *"graph is missing; run sync or rebuild"*.
   592	
   593	**My reading:** a faster cache would have returned the same stale entries. K2 (stale never answers) matters more here
   594	than any of the three stores.
   595	
   596	## What this changes elsewhere (proposed, not done)
   597	
   598	- `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` § Where it is put can keep `.cache/` as written. This document says when each part of it is built.

   600	## Not known
   601	
   602	- No trigger has a fixed number. Each is a comparison (B1, K3) that is measured once `project-memory/` has entries
   603	  and recorded questions; the measurement decides (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"this is design guide, how can I give you a
   604	  number?"*).
   605	- No measurement exists of any of the three on a project-memory corpus. All evidence is on other corpora: source code,
   606	  chat memory, enterprise documents.
   607	- The researchers' web reads went through extracts. `S-F*` quotes are not byte-checked (`sqlite-fts.claude.md`,
   608	  header). `V-F*` quotes were checked against saved copies.
   609	- The planted "tool that does not exist" check in the research was void: the names were readable in the run's
   610	  `state.md` (see that file's log).

   613

   615	reviewer stated it and is not averaged.
   616	
   617	| point | agy | codex | opencode |
   618	|---|---|---|---|
   619	| The BM-9 speed bound and the audit's `git ls-files` overhead | Holds codex's position: the bound holds. | The overhead biases grep's comparative timing, but removing it lowers grep's time and does not break the 15 ms bound; it should be disclosed. | The overhead makes the speed comparison unsafe and the finding undercuts BM-9. |
   620	| Corpus snapshot stability during the benchmark build | Holds codex's position: carry the warning; do not say no rerun happened. | A later run on the repaired harness is recorded, so "no rerun" is too strong; the builder's snapshot-stability warning is unclosed and should be carried. | The builder's note that the numbers must be re-run on a stable snapshot is unclosed; a rerun is needed. |
   621	| The audit's lower-severity findings | Sides with codex. | Not every remaining finding is a defect (one reports no problem), and the vector-deduplication finding does not change the hit rate. | The lower-severity findings remain open, and some of them bear on the text. |
   622	| Discovery versus exhaustive routes in the memory "none" rules | Adopts codex's position. | Discovery may use a derived cache; only exhaustive enumeration and proof of absence need a files-only route. | Keeps A's rows with citation fixes. |
