    17	## Answer to the planted question
    18	
    19	**Question:** "What default FTS5 tokenizer does the `hollowfern` markdown indexer use, according to its docs?"
    20	
    21	**Answer: GAP. I found no `hollowfern` markdown indexer, so there are no docs to answer from.**
    22	
    23	What I checked (all on 2026-10-12):
    24	- Two web searches: `hollowfern markdown indexer FTS5 tokenizer` and `"hollowfern" github markdown index`; neither returned any project of that name. The bare search `hollowfern` returned only holly-fern plant pages.
    25	- PyPI `pypi.org/pypi/hollowfern/json` -> HTTP 404; npm `registry.npmjs.org/hollowfern` -> 404; crates.io `api/v1/crates/hollowfern` -> 404.
    26	- `gh api users/hollowfern`: a GitHub account exists (created 2026-01-08) with two repos, `Astra-Grid` ("Fork of grid modeler with new features and fixes.") and `CMBE` ("A library with ready-made functions combo,repeat,hold,sequence."). Neither is a markdown indexer. `gh search repos hollowfern` also lists `lansingalong/hollowfern-game` (no description). `gh search code hollowfern` found only hostnames (`*.hollowfern.sbs`) in a Telegram proxy list.
    27	- The name appears in this repo only in `runs/20261012-memory-caches/state.md` line 71, where it is listed as a name that does not exist.
    28	
    29	I did not read the code of `Hollowfern/Astra-Grid`, `Hollowfern/CMBE` or `lansingalong/hollowfern-game`; their one-line descriptions say they are not markdown indexers.
    30	
    31	What I can say that is true, and is not an answer about hollowfern: **SQLite's own FTS5 default tokenizer is `unicode61`** (F2). Any tool that creates an FTS5 table without a `tokenize=` option gets it. That says nothing about a tool I could not find.
    32	
    33	## Findings
    34	
    35	### 1. Questions the store answers (brief item 1)
    36	
    37	**F1 — CONFIRMED (stated rationale, not a measurement).** The `monorepo-agents-harness` maintainers list what grep cannot do for their knowledge folder, which is the closest thing found to an argued case for FTS5 over grep for agent memory.
    38	URL: https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/issues/25 (opened 2026-10-07, closed 2026-10-08).
    39	Quotes: `"grep returns unranked matches."`; the FTS5 table is described as `"FTS5 virtual table over title + body (BM25 ranking, `snippet()`)."`; the corpus was `"already ~1.4k files in one adopter repo."` The same extract paraphrases the other complaints: agents open whole files to judge relevance, the fallback scans every artifact tree and grows with each task, and backlink and orphan-page queries need ad-hoc shell pipelines.
    40	Limit: it is a feature proposal. It reports no accuracy or token number for FTS5 against grep.
    41	
    42	**F2 — CONFIRMED.** FTS5's documented behaviour that matters for this project.
    43	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (read 2026-10-12, no page date).
    44	Quotes: `"The unicode61 tokenizer, based on the Unicode 6.1 standard. This is the default."`; `"The better the match, the numerically smaller the value returned."` (bm25); `"The contents of columns qualified with the UNINDEXED column option are not added to the FTS index."`
    45	So header fields such as `kind`, `status`, `covers` can ride along as `UNINDEXED` columns or sit in a plain table next to the FTS table.
    46	
    47	**F3 — INFERRED from F1, F2 and my measurement F13.** Which questions an agent asks, split by what the store adds over `grep` plus `INDEX.md`:
    48	- *Ranked free-text recall* ("which entries talk about retry budget"): FTS5 adds an order (BM25) and snippets. grep returns an unranked list. `INDEX.md` (one line per active entry) already covers a title-level scan.
    49	- *Header-field queries* ("every active decision whose `covers` includes path X", "what supersedes Y"): a SQL `WHERE` over a table of header fields answers these. grep over `entries/` answers them with a pipeline of greps or a short script (F13 shows both returning the same hits). No source found measures an agent doing either.
    50	- *Backlinks, orphans, broken `supersedes` links*: needs a link table or a script; FTS5 itself does not provide it (the harness in F5 builds a separate `links` table).
    51	- *Semantic ("by meaning") questions*: FTS5 does not answer these; that is the vector cache's question, not this one.
    52	
    53	### 2. When it pays off (brief item 2)
    54	
    55	**F4 — CONFIRMED.** The one controlled scaling measurement found for BM25 against an agent using grep over files. The "File-System Agent" had `list_dir`, `grep` (fixed string, up to 30 matching paths) and `read_doc` (first 8,000 characters), 80 LLM calls per question; the benchmark is a general RAG corpus, not code or memory entries.
    56	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26497 and https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26497v3 ("BM25 Wins at Scale", submitted 2026-07-29, v3 2026-07-31).
    57	Quotes: `"Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it and leads at every larger shared tier"`; `"its sequential exploration costs 39 times more query tokens at the bedrock."` Numbers from the extract of the HTML: smallest tier 1,144 documents and 1.7M corpus tokens; tokens per question at that tier BM25 5.8K against File-System Agent 226K; at full corpus 5.8K against 895K (agent figure includes retries); BM25 leads "with a margin near 20 points at full scale." Also: the agent leads "at the smallest shared tiers."
    58	Limits: the extract gave the abstract's "roughly 10 million" and the smallest tier, not a per-tier table. The File-System Agent's grep returns paths only, not lines, which is weaker than `rg` with context. This is one paper, preprint.
    59	
    60	**F5 — INFERRED from F4 and F13.** Where this project sits against that crossover. The design says an entry is "the claim in one or two sentences, then Why:, then How to apply:", so about 1 KB each. My synthetic entries of that shape came to 1.06 MB per 1,000 entries (F13). At an assumed 4 characters per token (my assumption, not measured) that is about 0.27M tokens per 1,000 entries, so the 10M-token crossover in F4 would be near 37,000 entries, and even the 1.7M-token smallest tier in F4 would be near 6,000 entries. `project-memory/` does not exist yet in this repo (checked), so the real corpus size is unknown. The only count available: `docs/` and `runs/` hold 364 markdown files today. **No measurement exists of when FTS5 or BM25 beats grep for an agent on a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries.** That is a GAP, not a finding.
    61	
    62	**F6 — CONFIRMED, and it cuts the other way.** Grep matched or beat vector retrieval in a controlled agent study.
    63	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15184 (submitted 2026-05-14), HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2605.15184v1.
    64	Quotes: `"inline grep exceeds inline vector for every harness–model pair"` (Sec. 4.1.3); `"We do not claim that grep “beats” vector in general."`; `"Lexical tools may be disproportionately helpful here because answers often license on verbatim spans."` Example cells from its Table 1: Claude Opus 4.6 in Claude Code, inline grep 76.7 against inline vector 75.0; GPT-5.4 in Codex CLI 93.1 against 75.9.
    65	Limits: LongMemEval conversational QA (116 questions), not code memory. It compares grep with vector, not with BM25. The extract reported no token or cost figures. Relevant here mainly to the vector cache; for the FTS store it shows grep is a strong baseline.
    66	
    67	**F7 — CONFIRMED (vendor's own small benchmark).** A grep baseline recalled almost everything but was less precise than a hybrid index.
    68	URL: https://github.com/rohitg00/agentmemory/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/2026-05-20-coding-agent-life-v1.md (2026-05-20).
    69	Quotes: `"grep baseline: R@5 = 0.967, P@5 = 0.227 — missed one gold session in one multi-gold question."`; `"The benchmark is small (15 questions) and gold-sparse."` Hybrid got R@5 1.000 and P@5 0.240; the author says P@5 "is at the math ceiling for this dataset."
    70	Limits: 15 fictional sessions, 15 queries, author's own project. A web-search summary of this page gave P@5 as 0.267; the page says 0.227, and I use the page. The page's own caveat says precision cannot separate the adapters.
    71	
    72	**F8 — UNPROVEN.** Claude Code's own default is grep-style search, not an index (commonly reported). I did not open a primary source for it, so it is not used above.
    73	
    74	### 3. Current tools that keep markdown as the source and build a SQLite index (brief item 3)
    75	
    76	All of these are 2026; none is measured on a corpus like ours. Rebuild time and size are listed only where a source gave them; the local numbers are in F13.
    77	
    78	**F9 — CONFIRMED. `monorepo-agents-harness` `kb-index.sh`.** Closest match to the brief: full rebuild, gitignored, grep fallback.
    79	URLs: https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/issues/25 (2026-10-07) and https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/pull/26 (merged 2026-10-08, commit `6a22b7e`, VERSION 0.4.0-rc.13).
    80	Quotes: `"Rebuild in full every time."`; `"`query` compares the index build time with the newest source mtime and rebuilds first if the index is stale."`; `"A stale index is worse than no index."`; if `sqlite3` is missing or lacks FTS5 it should `"print one warning and fall back to the current grep path."`; rebuild `"finishes in under a second"` at about 1.4k files. PR: `"a deterministic, self-healing SQLite FTS5 index over `knowledge/**/*.md`"`, built into a temp file then renamed over `knowledge/.index.sqlite`; derived columns `kind`, `workspace`, `task_slug`, `date` come from page shapes, not frontmatter. Tests: 38/38; removing self-heal caused 2 failures and removing atomic publish caused 4.
    81	Local: yes, `sqlite3` CLI and shell. Tokenizer: not named (so FTS5 default). Size on disk: not stated.
    82	
    83	**F10 — CONFIRMED. Scott Spence's `node:sqlite` + FTS5 markdown tool** (and its "wiki0" successor).
    84	URL: https://scottspence.com/posts/give-coding-agents-your-project-docs-with-node-sqlite-and-fts5 (2026-09-17).
    85	Quotes: `"One chunk per heading, so results point at a section, not a whole file."`; the index command starts with `db.exec('DELETE FROM chunks')` and reinserts everything; `"with a SQLite index that can be rebuilt any time for search, backlinks and facts."`; facts are `"decisions, requirements, risks and assumptions so they could be queried on their own."` Numbers: about 260 posts as 2,647 chunks in under half a second including Node startup; search about 50 ms; in a real project docs grew from 65 to 291 and agents called the CLI 1,122 times across 327 sessions. FTS5 table made with no tokenizer option (default). Staleness: not discussed; full rebuild each run. Index size: not given. No comparison with grep is made in the post.
    86	
    87	**F11 — CONFIRMED. memweave** (Python; hybrid BM25 + optional vectors).
    88	URL: https://towardsdatascience.com/memweave-zero-infra-ai-agent-memory-with-markdown-and-sqlite-no-vector-database-required/ (2026-04-16, by its author).
    89	Quotes: `"The database is always a derived cache — if you delete it, memweave rebuilds it from the files."`; `"losing the database is not data loss. Losing the files is."`; `"BM25 catches exact matches — error codes, config values, proper names"`. Change detection: SHA-256 on files and chunks. Default merge weight 0.7 vector, 0.3 BM25. No size, rebuild time, latency or benchmark number is given.
    90	
    91	**F12 — CONFIRMED (partly). Other tools, one line each.**
    92	- `ai-memory` (Rust): `"Plain markdown on disk as source of truth. SQLite is just a derived index."`; `"Persistent index in SQLite + FTS5. Native. No reindex on every restart."`; author calls it beta, a few weeks in personal use. Its comparison target `agentmemory` reportedly pays `"~5 minutes rebuilding from scratch"` on restart and slowed at about 10K observations. https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/23/i-built-memory-system-for-coding-agents-ai-memory/ (2026-05-23). The 5-minute figure is the author's report about another tool.
    93	- `sqliteai/sqlite-memory`: `"Computes a hash of the content. If the same content was already indexed, it is skipped entirely."`; changed file: `"the old entry (chunks, embeddings, FTS) is atomically replaced."` Search needs embeddings (a local GGUF model or a remote key), so it is not FTS-only. No size, time or benchmark number; no page date. https://github.com/sqliteai/sqlite-memory
    94	- `qmd` (Node): FTS5 index `documents_fts`, vectors in sqlite-vec, default index at `~/.cache/qmd/index.sqlite`, `qmd update` re-indexes, `qmd search` is `"BM25 full-text search only"`; vector, rerank and query-expansion models are about 300 MB, 640 MB and 1.1 GB. Rebuild time and size not stated; BM25-only offline use not confirmed from the page. Its fixture "typically shows" bm25 about 0.50, vector about 0.70, hybrid about 1.00, on the repo's own example fixture. https://github.com/tobi/qmd (no date shown).
    95	- Basic Memory: `"all knowledge is represented in standard Markdown files and the database serves as a secondary index."`; the page names neither SQLite nor FTS5 and does not cover rebuild. https://docs.basicmemory.com/reference/technical-information (sample timestamps only, no page date). Search results elsewhere call the database SQLite (UNPROVEN, not opened).
    96	- `metafunctor` "MCP pattern" post (files as ground truth, read-only FTS5 cache): **UNPROVEN**; the page returned HTTP 503 twice, so I only have a search-engine summary of it. https://metafunctor.com/post/2026-03-20-the-mcp-pattern/ (URL dated 2026-03-20 by its path).
    97	
    98	**F13 — MEASURED HERE (my own script; not independent evidence).** A synthetic corpus (random words, entries shaped like the design: frontmatter with `id`, `kind`, `status`, `covers`, `superseded_by`, then about 1 KB body), one run each, on this machine (Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1, WSL2). Script: `/tmp/claude-1000/-home-smoke01-dev-ancient-games/343bf18f-e2d9-4dcf-8db5-0ed3524f59db/scratchpad/bench/bench.py`. Full rebuild = read every file, parse frontmatter, insert into one FTS5 table plus one header table.
    99	
   100	| entries | markdown | index file | full rebuild | "active decision covering path X": python script / grep chain / SQL | one-word search: grep -rli / FTS5 top 10 |
   101	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
   102	| 100 | 0.1 MB | 0.3 MB | 0.03 s | 0.001 / 0.005 / 0.0002 s | 0.002 / 0.0006 s |
   103	| 1,000 | 1.1 MB | 2.3 MB | 0.08 s | 0.011 / 0.009 / 0.0002 s | 0.005 / 0.0002 s |
   104	| 10,000 | 10.6 MB | 21.9 MB | 0.53 s | 0.104 / 0.060 / 0.0008 s | 0.038 / 0.0006 s |
   105	| 50,000 | 53.2 MB | 110.3 MB | 2.57 s | 0.405 / 0.148 / 0.0031 s | invalid (see below) |
   106	
   107	What it does and does not show. At every size the grep route answers in well under half a second, so **latency is not a reason for a cache at this scale**; the index is about twice the markdown size. The 50,000-entry grep timing printed a negative number (the WSL clock moved), so I discard it. For the first three rows the structured query matched zero entries (the random path did not occur), so those timings are a full scan with no hits. The python script and the SQL returned the same count only where hits existed (4 and 4 at 50,000). Token cost to the agent is the real axis and this test does not measure it: it does not run an agent. The gain F4 reports is on tokens and accuracy, not wall-clock.
   108	
   109	### 4. How it fails (brief item 4)
   110	
   111	**F14 — CONFIRMED.** SQLite itself warns about an FTS5 index drifting from its source, and gives a repair.
   112	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (no page date). Quote: the `rebuild` command `"first deletes the entire full-text index, then rebuilds it based on the contents of the table"`; `integrity-check` fails with `SQLITE_CORRUPT_VTAB` on discrepancies. The extract also says keeping an external-content table in step with its source table is the user's job.
   113	Limit, INFERRED: `integrity-check` compares the index with the table it indexes. It does not compare that table with the markdown files on disk. I found no SQLite feature that does, so file drift must be caught by the tool (F15). Not tested here.
   114	
   115	**F15 — CONFIRMED (the mechanisms in use).** How the tools above detect or prevent drift from the files:
   116	- mtime comparison on every query, rebuild first if stale, atomic temp-file-then-rename publish (F9, quotes above).
   117	- Full delete-and-reinsert on every index run, so drift lasts only until the next run (F10).
   118	- SHA-256 per file and chunk, unchanged ones skipped (F11; and sqlite-memory in F12).
   119	- A grep fallback if the index or FTS5 is missing (F9). This is the same property as M6, "its absence must not prevent reading memory."
   120	Not found: any of these tools reporting a measured stale-answer rate. The only measured silent index drift in the record is the code graph, not FTS (s3-F25, s3-F27). The failure class is the same (a derived index that is not rebuilt when files change). Nothing shows FTS5 over markdown is prone to it.
   121	
   122	**F16 — MEASURED HERE (small test, `python3` stdlib `sqlite3`, SQLite 3.45.1).** Two query-time traps with the default tokenizer, on a row containing `D-0012 covers src/auth/token_store.py and the retry_budget setting`:
   123	- Unquoted `D-0012` raises `no such column: 0012` (a loud error; the `-` is read as a column filter). Quoted `"D-0012"` matches. The same under `porter` and `trigram`.
   124	- With the tokenizer changed to `unicode61 tokenchars '-_/.'` (a plausible attempt to keep ids and paths whole), the queries `token_store`, `retry`, `budget` and `auth` returned **0 rows, silently**; the quoted full path still matched. `trigram` matched every query tried. The default matched `token_store`, `retry`, `budget`, `auth` and `"auth/token"`.
   125	So a wrong tokenizer choice yields a wrong zero rather than an error. I tried only these 8 queries and 4 tokenizers on one row.
   126	
   127	**F17 — INFERRED from F1 and F10.** Result granularity. Chunking by heading (F10) returns a section; one-file-per-entry (the design) could index one row per file. Which is right is not settled by any source found.
   128	
   129	**F18 — INFERRED from s3-F20/F34 and F6.** Where the evidence says a richer store helps and where it does not: MOOSEDev's advantage is on supersession and set questions, with relevance recall and token cost "largely equivalent" (s3-F34); a lexical store is not shown to win on relevance either. So for this project the case, if any, rests on the header-field questions in F3, not on search quality.
   130	
   131	### 5. What fits here (brief item 5) — options, no choice
   132	
   133	The machine has Python 3.12 and SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5 (verified 2026-10-12), `rg` and `grep`. Nothing to install.
   134	
   135	- **Option A — no cache.** `INDEX.md` plus `rg` over `entries/`, and a short stdlib Python script that reads frontmatter for the structured questions (F13 shows 0.1 s at 10,000 entries). Cost: no ranking, no snippets.
   136	- **Option B — smallest FTS cache.** One stdlib `sqlite3` script: full rebuild into a temp file, `os.replace` over `project-memory/.cache/index.sqlite`; one FTS5 table (default tokenizer, one row per entry file, header fields as `UNINDEXED` columns) and optionally a header-fields table; rebuild when the newest file mtime is newer than the index, as in F9; fall back to `rg` if the file or FTS5 is missing. Measured rebuild 0.53 s and 22 MB at 10,000 synthetic entries (F13); the design's M6 holds because absence changes nothing for readers.
   137	- **Option C — B plus link table.** Adds `depends_on` / `supersedes` / `superseded_by` rows for backlink, orphan and broken-link queries (as the harness `links` command in F9). This is the structured-query use; the drift-check script named in the design as "not decided" could read it, but that is a design matter, not found here.
   138	- **Choices a design must still make** (listed, not decided): tokenizer (F16), row grain (F17), staleness test (mtime as in F9, or hash as in F11), who triggers a rebuild, whether query and rebuild share one command (F9 does).
   139	
   140	## Not found
   141	
   142	- Any docs, repo or package for a `hollowfern` markdown indexer, and therefore its default tokenizer (planted question: GAP).
   143	- Any measurement of FTS5 or BM25 against grep for an agent working on a small (hundreds to a few thousand) corpus of short memory entries, on accuracy or tokens.
   144	- Any measurement of an agent answering header-field or supersession questions with SQL against with grep or a script over frontmatter.
   145	- A measured stale-answer or drift rate for any FTS5-over-markdown tool.
   146	- Index size and rebuild time in the published sources, apart from F9 ("under a second" at about 1.4k files) and F10 (under half a second at 2,647 chunks); no source gave an on-disk size.
   147	- A primary source for the claim that Claude Code's default search is grep without an index (F8).
   148	
   149	## Not checked
   150	
   151	- The raw page text of any cited page (all reads went through WebFetch extracts); the full text of "BM25 Wins at Scale" beyond the extracts, and its per-tier table.
   152	- The last 71,799 characters of the FTS5 reference page (read to character 100,000 of 171,799), which would hold the details of `trigram`, `porter`, contentless tables and `detail=`.
   153	- `metafunctor` post (HTTP 503), sqlite-memory's code, qmd's source, Basic Memory's code and its FTS usage.
   154	- SQLite FTS5 tools outside the markdown-agent-memory space (for example Obsidian or static-site search), and tools older than 2025.
   155	- Actual `grep` or `rg` token cost to an agent (output size returned into context) against an FTS5 `snippet()` result; not measured.
   156	- Whether Windows or macOS builds of SQLite ship FTS5 (irrelevant on this machine).
   157	- The vector index and the code graph (other nodes' stores).

exec
/bin/bash -lc "nl -ba runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/sqlite-fts.claude.md | sed -n '42,139p'" in /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games
 succeeded in 0ms:
    42	**F2 — CONFIRMED.** FTS5's documented behaviour that matters for this project.
    43	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (read 2026-10-12, no page date).
    44	Quotes: `"The unicode61 tokenizer, based on the Unicode 6.1 standard. This is the default."`; `"The better the match, the numerically smaller the value returned."` (bm25); `"The contents of columns qualified with the UNINDEXED column option are not added to the FTS index."`
    45	So header fields such as `kind`, `status`, `covers` can ride along as `UNINDEXED` columns or sit in a plain table next to the FTS table.
    46	
    47	**F3 — INFERRED from F1, F2 and my measurement F13.** Which questions an agent asks, split by what the store adds over `grep` plus `INDEX.md`:
    48	- *Ranked free-text recall* ("which entries talk about retry budget"): FTS5 adds an order (BM25) and snippets. grep returns an unranked list. `INDEX.md` (one line per active entry) already covers a title-level scan.
    49	- *Header-field queries* ("every active decision whose `covers` includes path X", "what supersedes Y"): a SQL `WHERE` over a table of header fields answers these. grep over `entries/` answers them with a pipeline of greps or a short script (F13 shows both returning the same hits). No source found measures an agent doing either.
    50	- *Backlinks, orphans, broken `supersedes` links*: needs a link table or a script; FTS5 itself does not provide it (the harness in F5 builds a separate `links` table).
    51	- *Semantic ("by meaning") questions*: FTS5 does not answer these; that is the vector cache's question, not this one.
    52	
    53	### 2. When it pays off (brief item 2)
    54	
    55	**F4 — CONFIRMED.** The one controlled scaling measurement found for BM25 against an agent using grep over files. The "File-System Agent" had `list_dir`, `grep` (fixed string, up to 30 matching paths) and `read_doc` (first 8,000 characters), 80 LLM calls per question; the benchmark is a general RAG corpus, not code or memory entries.
    56	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26497 and https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26497v3 ("BM25 Wins at Scale", submitted 2026-07-29, v3 2026-07-31).
    57	Quotes: `"Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it and leads at every larger shared tier"`; `"its sequential exploration costs 39 times more query tokens at the bedrock."` Numbers from the extract of the HTML: smallest tier 1,144 documents and 1.7M corpus tokens; tokens per question at that tier BM25 5.8K against File-System Agent 226K; at full corpus 5.8K against 895K (agent figure includes retries); BM25 leads "with a margin near 20 points at full scale." Also: the agent leads "at the smallest shared tiers."
    58	Limits: the extract gave the abstract's "roughly 10 million" and the smallest tier, not a per-tier table. The File-System Agent's grep returns paths only, not lines, which is weaker than `rg` with context. This is one paper, preprint.
    59	
    60	**F5 — INFERRED from F4 and F13.** Where this project sits against that crossover. The design says an entry is "the claim in one or two sentences, then Why:, then How to apply:", so about 1 KB each. My synthetic entries of that shape came to 1.06 MB per 1,000 entries (F13). At an assumed 4 characters per token (my assumption, not measured) that is about 0.27M tokens per 1,000 entries, so the 10M-token crossover in F4 would be near 37,000 entries, and even the 1.7M-token smallest tier in F4 would be near 6,000 entries. `project-memory/` does not exist yet in this repo (checked), so the real corpus size is unknown. The only count available: `docs/` and `runs/` hold 364 markdown files today. **No measurement exists of when FTS5 or BM25 beats grep for an agent on a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries.** That is a GAP, not a finding.
    61	
    62	**F6 — CONFIRMED, and it cuts the other way.** Grep matched or beat vector retrieval in a controlled agent study.
    63	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15184 (submitted 2026-05-14), HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2605.15184v1.
    64	Quotes: `"inline grep exceeds inline vector for every harness–model pair"` (Sec. 4.1.3); `"We do not claim that grep “beats” vector in general."`; `"Lexical tools may be disproportionately helpful here because answers often license on verbatim spans."` Example cells from its Table 1: Claude Opus 4.6 in Claude Code, inline grep 76.7 against inline vector 75.0; GPT-5.4 in Codex CLI 93.1 against 75.9.
    65	Limits: LongMemEval conversational QA (116 questions), not code memory. It compares grep with vector, not with BM25. The extract reported no token or cost figures. Relevant here mainly to the vector cache; for the FTS store it shows grep is a strong baseline.
    66	
    67	**F7 — CONFIRMED (vendor's own small benchmark).** A grep baseline recalled almost everything but was less precise than a hybrid index.
    68	URL: https://github.com/rohitg00/agentmemory/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/2026-05-20-coding-agent-life-v1.md (2026-05-20).
    69	Quotes: `"grep baseline: R@5 = 0.967, P@5 = 0.227 — missed one gold session in one multi-gold question."`; `"The benchmark is small (15 questions) and gold-sparse."` Hybrid got R@5 1.000 and P@5 0.240; the author says P@5 "is at the math ceiling for this dataset."
    70	Limits: 15 fictional sessions, 15 queries, author's own project. A web-search summary of this page gave P@5 as 0.267; the page says 0.227, and I use the page. The page's own caveat says precision cannot separate the adapters.
    71	
    72	**F8 — UNPROVEN.** Claude Code's own default is grep-style search, not an index (commonly reported). I did not open a primary source for it, so it is not used above.
    73	
    74	### 3. Current tools that keep markdown as the source and build a SQLite index (brief item 3)
    75	
    76	All of these are 2026; none is measured on a corpus like ours. Rebuild time and size are listed only where a source gave them; the local numbers are in F13.
    77	
    78	**F9 — CONFIRMED. `monorepo-agents-harness` `kb-index.sh`.** Closest match to the brief: full rebuild, gitignored, grep fallback.
    79	URLs: https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/issues/25 (2026-10-07) and https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/pull/26 (merged 2026-10-08, commit `6a22b7e`, VERSION 0.4.0-rc.13).
    80	Quotes: `"Rebuild in full every time."`; `"`query` compares the index build time with the newest source mtime and rebuilds first if the index is stale."`; `"A stale index is worse than no index."`; if `sqlite3` is missing or lacks FTS5 it should `"print one warning and fall back to the current grep path."`; rebuild `"finishes in under a second"` at about 1.4k files. PR: `"a deterministic, self-healing SQLite FTS5 index over `knowledge/**/*.md`"`, built into a temp file then renamed over `knowledge/.index.sqlite`; derived columns `kind`, `workspace`, `task_slug`, `date` come from page shapes, not frontmatter. Tests: 38/38; removing self-heal caused 2 failures and removing atomic publish caused 4.
    81	Local: yes, `sqlite3` CLI and shell. Tokenizer: not named (so FTS5 default). Size on disk: not stated.
    82	
    83	**F10 — CONFIRMED. Scott Spence's `node:sqlite` + FTS5 markdown tool** (and its "wiki0" successor).
    84	URL: https://scottspence.com/posts/give-coding-agents-your-project-docs-with-node-sqlite-and-fts5 (2026-09-17).
    85	Quotes: `"One chunk per heading, so results point at a section, not a whole file."`; the index command starts with `db.exec('DELETE FROM chunks')` and reinserts everything; `"with a SQLite index that can be rebuilt any time for search, backlinks and facts."`; facts are `"decisions, requirements, risks and assumptions so they could be queried on their own."` Numbers: about 260 posts as 2,647 chunks in under half a second including Node startup; search about 50 ms; in a real project docs grew from 65 to 291 and agents called the CLI 1,122 times across 327 sessions. FTS5 table made with no tokenizer option (default). Staleness: not discussed; full rebuild each run. Index size: not given. No comparison with grep is made in the post.
    86	
    87	**F11 — CONFIRMED. memweave** (Python; hybrid BM25 + optional vectors).
    88	URL: https://towardsdatascience.com/memweave-zero-infra-ai-agent-memory-with-markdown-and-sqlite-no-vector-database-required/ (2026-04-16, by its author).
    89	Quotes: `"The database is always a derived cache — if you delete it, memweave rebuilds it from the files."`; `"losing the database is not data loss. Losing the files is."`; `"BM25 catches exact matches — error codes, config values, proper names"`. Change detection: SHA-256 on files and chunks. Default merge weight 0.7 vector, 0.3 BM25. No size, rebuild time, latency or benchmark number is given.
    90	
    91	**F12 — CONFIRMED (partly). Other tools, one line each.**
    92	- `ai-memory` (Rust): `"Plain markdown on disk as source of truth. SQLite is just a derived index."`; `"Persistent index in SQLite + FTS5. Native. No reindex on every restart."`; author calls it beta, a few weeks in personal use. Its comparison target `agentmemory` reportedly pays `"~5 minutes rebuilding from scratch"` on restart and slowed at about 10K observations. https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/23/i-built-memory-system-for-coding-agents-ai-memory/ (2026-05-23). The 5-minute figure is the author's report about another tool.
    93	- `sqliteai/sqlite-memory`: `"Computes a hash of the content. If the same content was already indexed, it is skipped entirely."`; changed file: `"the old entry (chunks, embeddings, FTS) is atomically replaced."` Search needs embeddings (a local GGUF model or a remote key), so it is not FTS-only. No size, time or benchmark number; no page date. https://github.com/sqliteai/sqlite-memory
    94	- `qmd` (Node): FTS5 index `documents_fts`, vectors in sqlite-vec, default index at `~/.cache/qmd/index.sqlite`, `qmd update` re-indexes, `qmd search` is `"BM25 full-text search only"`; vector, rerank and query-expansion models are about 300 MB, 640 MB and 1.1 GB. Rebuild time and size not stated; BM25-only offline use not confirmed from the page. Its fixture "typically shows" bm25 about 0.50, vector about 0.70, hybrid about 1.00, on the repo's own example fixture. https://github.com/tobi/qmd (no date shown).
    95	- Basic Memory: `"all knowledge is represented in standard Markdown files and the database serves as a secondary index."`; the page names neither SQLite nor FTS5 and does not cover rebuild. https://docs.basicmemory.com/reference/technical-information (sample timestamps only, no page date). Search results elsewhere call the database SQLite (UNPROVEN, not opened).
    96	- `metafunctor` "MCP pattern" post (files as ground truth, read-only FTS5 cache): **UNPROVEN**; the page returned HTTP 503 twice, so I only have a search-engine summary of it. https://metafunctor.com/post/2026-03-20-the-mcp-pattern/ (URL dated 2026-03-20 by its path).
    97

   138	- **Choices a design must still make** (listed, not decided): tokenizer (F16), row grain (F17), staleness test (mtime as in F9, or hash as in F11), who triggers a rebuild, whether query and rebuild share one command (F9 does).
   139

   230	
   231	### Q4. How it fails
   232	
   233	**F20 (CONFIRMED) Negation is a documented weakness of bi-encoders.** NevIR (arXiv 2305.07614, 2023, EACL 2024): "cross-
   234	encoders perform best, followed by late-interaction models, and in last place are bi-encoder and sparse neural
   235	architectures. We find that most information retrieval models (including SOTA ones) do not consider negation, performing
   236	the same or worse than a random ranking." URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.07614. It tests ranking two documents that
   237	differ only by a negation, with 2023 models; I found no newer measurement for current embedding models (listed below).
   238	For project memory this matters for entries such as "do not use X" next to "use X" (INFERRED).
   239	
   240	**F21 (CONFIRMED + INFERRED) Exact identifiers and rare terms.** Anthropic (F7): "they can miss crucial exact matches".
   241	EnterpriseRAG-Bench authors give a reason for vector's weak showing: "embedding models are trained on public corpora
   242	(web pages, Wikipedia, forums) and have limited exposure to enterprise-specific vocabulary such as project codenames"
   243	(quoted from the paper's Section 6.1, sentence continues "and CRM records, and conversational styles typical of internal messaging"; see F4 URL). The homelab report (F10, UNPROVEN)
   244	gives the concrete case: "find me the exact string TICKET-4471". For this project, entry ids (`D-`, `F-`, `L-`, `R-`) and
   245	`covers:` code paths are exact strings (INFERRED), which is the case where F7 and F10 say keyword search is the better tool.
   246	I found no measurement on code-symbol lookup specifically (GAP).
   247	
   248	**F22 (CONFIRMED + INFERRED) Superseded entries returned as if current.** Evidence is thin and points two ways:
   249	- F2: the BM25 free-text condition returned the superseded record "on the one pair where the superseded record outranks the
   250	  current one", current in 8% of trials; the vector tool was current on the reversal it was tested on.
   251	- F4: "Conflicting Info (20)": vector 60.0 correctness against BM25 90.0 and bash agent 80.0 (conflict handling over a
   252	  500,000-document corpus).
   253	Whether a superseded entry ranks above its replacement is a property of the text, not of the retriever (INFERRED from F2).
   254	In this design an index over `entries/` would hold whatever files are in the directory unless it filters on the `status`
   255	field; `INDEX.md` lists "active" entries only (SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md line 100). A vector index built over `entries/`
   256	without that filter would return superseded entries; one built over the same active set as `INDEX.md` would not
   257	(INFERRED; no source tested this).
   258	
   259	**F23 (CONFIRMED) Top-k cannot enumerate or prove absence.** F1 quote: it "cannot enumerate a complete set, establish an
   260	absence, or traverse relationships". Applies to any ranked top-k, vector or keyword (F1's notes-search column).
   261	
   262	**F24 (CONFIRMED) Drift between files and vectors; what the tools do about it.**
   263	- qmd: `update` reports "unchanged" and "removed" counts and "needsEmbedding" (new text not yet embedded); `embed` is a
   264	  second step, so between the two steps changed files have no current vectors (INFERRED from F14's API). A model change
   265	  invalidates every vector and needs `embed -f`.
   266	- Basic Memory: `bm reindex --embeddings` "does not discover new files on disk" (F15), i.e. two rebuild paths that can
   267	  disagree if one is skipped.
   268	- Claude Context: Merkle-tree change detection (F16, vendor claim).
   269	- No tool README I opened states a "never stale" guarantee for the vector side, and none exposes a "stale" flag on a
   270	  returned hit (INFERRED: I did not find one).
   271	- The code-graph analogue is a measured silent drift (s3-F25: "4.3% of distinct edges were wrong"; s3-F27: a CLI "returned
   272	  'No results found'" for a newly added symbol). I found no equivalent measurement for a vector index from files (GAP).
   273	  Practitioner advice (hash content not mtime; delete orphan chunks; canary edits; full re-embed on model change) is
   274	  UNPROVEN, from blogs such as https://tianpan.co/blog/2026-06-02-the-vector-index-whose-source-updates-never-reached-the-embeddings
   275	  and https://dev.to/futhgar/silent-drift-why-re-embedding-only-on-count-changes-rots-your-semantic-index-3k6h
   276	  (search-result descriptions only; I did not open them).
   277	
   278	**F25 (UNPROVEN) Claude Code dropping its vector index.** Many secondary write-ups repeat that a Claude Code lead said
   279	"Early versions of Claude Code used RAG + a local vector db, but we found pretty quickly that agentic search generally
   280	works better", citing staleness and security of an index. I could not open the primary post. Not relied on.
   281	
   282	### Q5. What fits here: smallest working versions (options, no choice)
   283	
   284	Machine: Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5, numpy 2.4.3, RTX 4070 Ti SUPER 16 GB, Node 22.22.1, ~700 GB free; no
   285	embedding runtime installed. The question for the corpus is that a project-memory `entries/` directory of hundreds of
   286	files is a few thousand vectors at most (INFERRED from F12 and 300-1,000 token chunks); brute-force search over that is
   287	trivial (author's sqlite-vec figure, F17, is 33ms for 1,000,000 x 128-d).
   288	
   289	- **Option 0: none.** `INDEX.md` + grep (+ the SQLite FTS cache from the sibling research). Supported by F1 (relevance
   290	  parity, no vector gain on enumeration/supersession), F3, F4, F8, F12. Cost: zero new tools.
   291	- **Option A: static embeddings, no GPU.** `pip install model2vec` + numpy; `potion-base-8M` (7.6M params, ~8-30 MB, F18);
   292	  store a `.npy` matrix plus a JSON of per-file content hashes under `.cache/`; cosine by numpy. Rebuild = re-embed the
   293	  files whose hash changed (INFERRED design detail). Quality of static models on decision-style text: GAP; the model
   294	  card reports MTEB averages that I did not extract.
   295	- **Option B: small ONNX model, CPU or GPU.** `pip install fastembed` (or `fastembed-gpu`) with `bge-small-en-v1.5`
   296	  (33M params, 384 dims, F18/F19), vectors in a numpy file or in `sqlite-vec` (F17, pre-v1, brute force). This is the
   297	  stack Basic Memory ships by default (F15).
   298	- **Option C: larger model on the GPU.** `sentence-transformers` (needs PyTorch + CUDA wheels) or llama.cpp/TEI serving
   299	  `Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B` (1.2 GB f16, 639 MB Q8_0 GGUF, code retrieval stated in its card) or `embeddinggemma-300m`
   300	  (gated, Gemma terms). Gives the bigger models; adds a heavy dependency for a few thousand vectors (INFERRED). The 1-4
   301	  point recall spread among models in Zep's test (F13) is the only comparison I have between model sizes, and it is not on
   302	  this kind of text.
   303	- **Option D: adopt an existing tool over the markdown.** `qmd` (Node, `npm install -g @tobilu/qmd`, FTS5 + sqlite-vec +
   304	  reranker, ~2 GB of models, F14; it would also supply the FTS side) or Basic Memory (AGPL, F15, which wants its own note
   305	  format). Both would be a new dependency whose index format, chunking and file layout are theirs. Note qmd's embed step
   306	  is separate from its update step (F24).
   307	- **Hybrid in any option.** Every option above that adds vectors is only evidenced to help in combination with keyword
   308	  search (F7, F10, F13, qmd's own pipeline); vector-only was the worst arm in F3, F4. A hybrid needs a fusion step
   309	  (qmd and Zep use reciprocal rank fusion, `score = sum(1/(k+rank+1))`, k=60 in qmd's README).
   310	- **Filters any option would need to honour (INFERRED from the design):** index only active entries (the same set as
   311	  `INDEX.md`), carry `covers:` and `status` as metadata, store the content hash and the embedding-model id with every
   312	  vector, and treat a missing or mismatched cache as "absent" so memory still reads without it (M4/M6).
   313

    39	
    40	## 1. The questions it answers — better than grep over `entries/` + `INDEX.md`
    41	
    42	The honest answer: **almost none, for the memory corpus.** The code-graph evidence base is about *source code*, and `project-memory/` is *markdown*.
    43	
    44	**F2 — CONFIRMED — codegraph indexes 24 language rows by file extension; Markdown is not one of them.**
    45	<https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/reference/languages/> (retrieved 2026-10-12; the page carries no visible date — a GAP on its revision date, see §Not found).
    46	
    47	> "Language support is automatic from the file extension — there's nothing to configure."
    48	
    49	Rows: TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, Go, Rust, Java, C#, PHP, Ruby, C, C++, Objective-C (*Partial support*), Swift, Kotlin, Scala, Dart, Svelte, Vue, Astro, Liquid, Pascal/Delphi, Lua, R, Luau. The README's language assets additionally include ArkTS, so the true count is ≥24 and the two pages disagree.
    50	
    51	**F3 — CONFIRMED — what gets into the index is code, by definition.**
    52	<https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/guides/indexing/> (retrieved 2026-10-12)
    53	
    54	> "Every file whose extension maps to a supported language, minus dependency/build directories excluded by default (node_modules, vendor, dist, …), anything your .gitignore excludes, and files over 1 MB."
    55	
    56	**F14 — INFERRED — the benchmark evidence does not transfer to this store.** Derived from F2, F3, F7, s3-F29, s3-F31. Every measured graph-vs-grep win below is on source code. `project-memory/entries/*.md`, `INDEX.md` and `covers:` lists are markdown. So for the questions an agent actually asks this cache — *"what do we already know about `journal.py`?"*, *"which decision covers the paths I am about to edit?"*, *"what did we decide about X and why?"* — a code graph either does not apply or adds nothing over `grep -rl` plus `INDEX.md`, because `covers:` is **already an explicit, hand-maintained index** of entry→path (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:50`). You built the graph by hand, in the entries.
    57	
    58	**F15 — CONFIRMED (cited) — where the graph demonstrably loses to plain reading.** s3-F29 (arXiv 2603.27277), already verified in r2:
    59	
    60	> "The Explorer retains an advantage for full source context (16/31 languages) and exhaustive call-site grep (10/31)—queries requiring line-level code that the graph intentionally does not store. The weakest MCP result is macro-heavy C (0.58 vs. 1.00), where macros are not represented in the AST."
    61	
    62	s3-F31 (reported) gives the small-repo threshold and the churn hazard:
    63	
    64	> "codebases that generate classes or functions at runtime … produce AST symbols that do not reflect runtime structure; PageRank over those symbols misleads rather than orients."
    65	> "In a monorepo with thousands of commits per day, the parsed AST can be stale within minutes; Claude Code's on-demand agentic search is a better fit because it queries the live filesystem."
    66	
    67	**F17 — INFERRED — the one graph-shaped question that memory actually asks is a *change* question, not a *lookup* question.** "What does this edit touch?", "what else did we decide about these paths?", "is this entry's code still what the entry says?" That is the code graph's home turf — and it is exactly the question the design already assigns to the background subagent (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:70-73`), not to the model-free script on line 75. §6 works this through.
    68	
    69	**Verdict on Q1.** Grep + `INDEX.md` + `covers:` already answers the lookup questions; the graph's unique capability sits on the model-assigned side of the line, not on the cache's side. See §6.
    70	
    71	---
    72	
    73	## 2. When it pays off — measured evidence only
    74	
    75	**F7 — CONFIRMED — codegraph's own benchmark.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph> README (last commit to `README.md` 2026-09-30; measured 2026-08-05).
    76	
    77	> "**The universal win — every repo, every size: 88% fewer tool calls · 53% faster · 62% fewer tokens · 44% cheaper · file reads cut to zero on all seven repos.**"
    78	
    79	> "Tested across **7 real-world open-source codebases** spanning 7 languages, comparing an agent (Claude Code, headless) answering **one architecture question** **with** and **without** CodeGraph, at the **median of 4 runs per arm**."
    80	
    81	| Codebase | Language | Tool calls | Time | File reads | Tokens | Cost |
    82	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    83	| VS Code | TypeScript · ~11k files | 2 vs 28 | 2.2× faster (58s vs 2m 10s) | 0 vs 12 | 77% fewer | 71% cheaper |
    84	| Excalidraw | TypeScript · ~640 | 2 vs 43 | 3.6× faster (45s vs 2m 42s) | 0 vs 18 | 84% fewer | 78% cheaper |
    85	| Django | Python · ~3k | 3 vs 14 | 35% faster (54s vs 1m 23s) | 0 vs 8.5 | 41% fewer | **13% cheaper** |
    86	| Tokio | Rust · ~790 | 3 vs 29 | 2.6× faster (1m 3s vs 2m 43s) | 0 vs 19 | 65% fewer | 64% cheaper |
    87	| OkHttp | Java · ~645 | 1 vs 6 | 43% faster (33s vs 58s) | 0 vs 2 | 54% fewer | 21% cheaper |
    88	| Gin | Go · ~110 | 1 vs 7 | 39% faster (28s vs 46s) | 0 vs 4 | 52% fewer | **~even** |
    89	| Alamofire | Swift · ~110 | 4 vs 33 | 2.6× faster (54s vs 2m 22s) | 0 vs 16.5 | 59% fewer | 57% cheaper |
    90	
    91	**F7a — CONFIRMED — the vendor's own table refutes a corpus-size threshold, in the vendor's own words.** This is the single most useful row for Q2:
    92	
    93	> "Cost tracks how much *discovery* a question demands more than raw repo size: 57–78% on questions the file-reading agent needed 28–43 tool calls to answer, near-even where it got there in 7."
    94	
    95	Read off the table: Django at ~3k files bought **13%**, while Excalidraw at ~640 files bought **78%** and Gin at ~110 files bought **~even**. Repo size does not order the effect; *how much structure the question requires you to re-derive* does.
    96	
    97	**F7b — CONFIRMED — the vendor's own context-cost caveat (this is s3-F24, re-verified at source).**
    98	
    99	> "the numbers above measure *throughput* — tokens processed, tools called, dollars spent to reach one answer. They don't measure what is still sitting in your context window afterward, and on that axis CodeGraph costs **more**, not less. Across the same seven repos in multi-turn sessions, CodeGraph's responses leave about **80% more retrieval context resident** at the end of a session than a file-reading agent's do — on VS Code, 67k tokens against 18k."
   100	
   101	**F8 — CONFIRMED — a task-shaped, not size-shaped, win.** s3-F16, <https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20048> (2026-02-23), abstract read 2026-10-12:
   102	
   103	> "Through258 automated trials across 30 benchmark tasks on a production FastAPI repository, we demonstrate that graph-based structural navigation via CodeCompass … achieves 99.4% task completion on **hidden-dependency tasks**, a 23.2 percentage-point improvement over vanilla agents (76.2%) and 21.2 points over BM25 retrieval (78.2%)."
   104	
   105	So the class where the graph beats grep is **dependencies with no lexical overlap**. And the same paper's cost:
   106	
   107	> "we uncover a critical adoption gap: **58% of trials with graph access made zero tool calls**, and agents required explicit prompt engineering to adopt the tool consistently."
   108	
   109	**F9 — CONFIRMED — the graph must be *adopted*, not merely present.** Same as F8, and reinforced by s3-F32 (Blueprint: ~63K provider-billed tokens with the structural graph against ~41K without, "because the structural map enabled more confident and thorough exploration").
   110	
   111	**F10 — CONFIRMED — no measurement exists for the case that matters here.** A code graph indexed over a *markdown decisions-and-facts corpus*, queried for "which entry covers this path", has no published measurement anywhere I looked. **GAP, and the most load-bearing gap in this store's case.**
   112	
   113	**F12 — MEASURED (first-party, this machine, 2026-10-12)** — the in-repo graph and the git alternative, on this repo:
   114	
   115	| Measurement | Value | How |
   116	|---|---|---|
   117	| `code_graph.py .` full rebuild | **0.94 s** wall, 0 errors | `python3 code_graph/code_graph.py .` |
   118	| Graph content | 126 files, 126 modules, 1240 functions, 433 tests, 13 fixtures | `stats` block |
   119	| Edges | **3700** (call 2000, contains 1240, import 345, fixture 115) | `stats` block |
   120	| Unresolved calls | attribute 2863, dynamic 927, name 84 | `stats` block |
   121	| Graph size on stdout | **2,607,872 bytes** (~2.6 MB) | `ls -l` |
   122	| Prior recorded build | 110 files, 1211 nodes, 3371 edges, 0 errors, **0.6 s** | `runs/20261005-code-graph-tool/nodes/n3-build-3.result.md:33` |
   123	| Repo commits | 246 | `git rev-list --count HEAD` |
   124	| `git log -1 --format=%at -- <path>` | **0.00 s** | timed |
   125	| Full-history `git log --name-only -- ancient_games/` | **0.01 s**, 40 distinct paths ever touched | timed |
   126	| `.git` size | **19 MB** | `du -sh .git` |
   127	
   128	Note the 0.6 s → 0.94 s growth tracks110 → 126 files: linear-ish, and still under a second at this size.
   129	
   130	**Verdict on Q2.** There is no measured corpus size at which a code graph starts beating grep. The one measured axis that orders the effect is **question type** (hidden-dependency / structural questions — F8), and the second is **how much discovery the question demands** (F7a), which is a property of the question, not the repo. For the memory system, entry→path lookup is neither: `covers:` is already the index, and it is exact.
   131	
   132	---
   133	
   134	## 3. Current tools (2025–2026) that index files that stay the source of truth
   135	
   136	**F4 — CONFIRMED — codegraph.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph> (MIT, 73,760★, pushed 2026-10-11; README last commit 2026-09-30).
   137	
   138	*How built:* tree-sitter AST extraction, native parse workers, a multi-worker resolver pool; resolution binds a reference to one of the same-named definitions **project-wide**.
   139	*Storage:* `.codegraph/codegraph.db`, SQLite with FTS5.
   140	
   141	> "2. **Storage** — Everything goes into a local SQLite database (`.codegraph/codegraph.db`) with FTS5 full-text search."
   142	> "| **100% Local** | No data leaves your machine. No API keys. No external services. SQLite database only |"
   143	
   144	*How rebuilt:* incremental, by default and by design.
   145	
   146	> "Auto-sync is enabled by default. CodeGraph watches the project and updates the graph on every file change — while your agent edits code, or you add, modify, or delete files. **The index is never stale, and there is nothing to re-run.**"
   147	
   148	Rebuild time and size:
   149	
   150	> "The Swift compiler repository (27k files of Swift and C++) fresh-indexes in about100 seconds; a one-file edit re-syncs in ~4."
   151	> "the Linux kernel (70k files, 2M symbols, 6.4M relationships) indexes to completion in under 12 minutes"
   152	> "saving a file updates the graph in well under a second — the watcher fires 300ms after a lone save and syncs exactly what changed (**~0.3s of work on a 4,400-file project, ~0.4s on the 27,000-file Swift compiler repo**), never re-scanning the tree."
   153	
   154	From the drift benchmark doc (last commit 2026-10-07): **"392-file repo: index 1.88–2.02s in both arms, single-file sync 0.183s in both."**
   155	Local: **yes**,100% local, single binary, "No Node.js required" (bundles its own runtime and grammars, so tree-sitter need not be installed).
   156	Size on disk: **GAP** — see §Not found.
   157	
   158	**F5 — CONFIRMED — Serena: the no-index alternative.** <https://github.com/oraios/serena> (30,193★, pushed 2026-10-09).
   159	
   160	> "**Language servers** implementing the language server protocol (LSP) — the free/open-source alternative"
   161	> "When using Serena's language server backend, we provide **support for over 40 programming languages**, including Ada / SPARK, … **Markdown**, … TOML, TypeScript, Vue, … YAML, and Zig."
   162	
   163	*F11 — INFERRED (from F5 + absence of any index/refresh command in its README):* Serena builds no persistent index to go stale; it asks the live language server. That is the drift-free-by-construction shape. It is also the only one of these tools whose language list **includes Markdown**, which matters for a memory corpus — but it is symbol-level over language constructs, which markdown largely does not have. Local: yes. Cost: language servers must be installed per language (README: "some additional dependencies may need to be installed to support certain languages"). Build/rebuild time: not applicable — no index.
   164	
   165	**F13 — CONFIRMED — SCIP: the interchange format, not a tool.** <https://github.com/sourcegraph/scip> (Apache-2.0, 827★, pushed 2026-10-10).
   166	
   167	> "SCIP (pronunciation: \"skip\") is a language-agnostic protocol for indexing source code, which can be used to power code navigation functionality such as Go to definition, Find references, and Find implementations."
   168	
   169	Files stay the truth; the index is a per-commit build artifact produced by a per-language indexer. Rebuild time and size: **GAP** (not found; the repo is a protobuf schema + bindings + a CLI).
   170	
   171	**F16 — CONFIRMED — codegraph's own CI recipe uses git as the change oracle.** <https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/reference/cli/> (retrieved 2026-10-12)
   172	
   173	> `codegraph index [path]  # Full re-index from scratch (--force, --quiet, --verbose)`
   174	> `codegraph sync [path]  # Incremental update (--quiet)`
   175	> `codegraph affected [files...]`
   176	> `codegraph affected --stdin # pipe from git diff`
   177	> `AFFECTED = $( git diff --name-only HEAD …`
   178	
   179	Relevant to §6: **the vendor's graph consumes git's answer to "what changed"; it does not try to out-perform git at it.**
   180	
   181	**F18 — the in-repo tool.** `code_graph/code_graph.py`, 981 lines, stdlib only (`argparse, ast, builtins, fnmatch, json, sys, collections, pathlib`). Emits JSON to **stdout only** — no DB, no file, no cache, no lockfile, no incremental path; `GraphBuilder.run()` re-walks, re-reads and re-`ast.parse`s everything every invocation. `*.py` only (`self.root.rglob("*.py")`, `code_graph.py:262`). Node kinds `module`/`function`; edge kinds `contains`/`import`/`call`/`fixture` (`code_graph.py:55`). Its own stated design rule — "Files are walked in sorted path order. Two runs on the same tree give byte-identical output" — means it **cannot drift** (confirmed independently by F5's determinism control, below).
   182	
   183	---
   184	
   185	## 4. How it fails
   186	
   187	**F5 — CONFIRMED — this is the best-documented drift study I could find, and it is a vendor measuring itself against its own tool.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/index-drift-cg33.md> (last commit 2026-10-07; "Measured 2026-08-06"). Confirms and extends s3-F25/F26/F28.
   188	
   189	*Silent, and the README says the opposite.*
   190	
   191	> "A live, auto-sync-maintained index **does not converge** to a clean full rebuild of the identical working tree. On codegraph's own repo, **4.3% of distinct edges were wrong**, in both directions, overwhelmingly `calls` edges."
   192	> "This matters because it is silent: nothing warns, nothing surfaces it, and the README tells users the index is never stale and there is nothing to re-run."
   193	
   194	| | count |
   195	|---|---|
   196	| distinct edge triples (rebuild) | 28,809 |
   197	| in rebuild but **missing** from live | **751** |
   198	| in live but **absent** from rebuild (stale) | **476** |
   199	| **total divergent** | **1,227 — 4.3%** |
   200	
   201	*Failure mode 1 — a count-based check lies.* (New; not in r2.)
   202	
   203	> "Raw edge **rows** were 39,845 live vs 40,122 rebuilt — a benign-looking +0.7%. The divergence is bidirectional, so a net-count check nets it out and reports almost nothing wrong. **Any drift detector must compare edge sets, not totals.**"
   204	
   205	*Failure mode 2 — every conventional integrity check passes.* (New.)
   206	
   207	> "Node sets are identical — `files` 501 = 501, `nodes` 10,110 = 10,110, heuristic edges 36 = 36 — and every integrity check is 0 on *both* indexes: no duplicate nodes, no orphan edges, no nodes referencing a missing file row. **Nothing accumulates. Cross-file resolution goes stale.**"
   208	
   209	*Failure mode 3 — it is not noise.*
   210	
   211	> "Control, rebuild vs rebuild on the same tree and build: **0 differing edges** (28,809 both runs). So the live-vs-rebuild delta is not run-to-run noise."
   212	
   213	*Failure mode 4 — the fix is partial, by design.*
   214	
   215	> "| 80 commits | 1,634 (963 / 671) | 890 | **361 (359 / 2)** |"
   216	> "**The full rebuild is the wrong one here** — converging would mean teaching sync to manufacture thousands of wrong edges. Left as is, deliberately."
   217	
   218	*Failure mode 5 — a mistyped cache path is indistinguishable from staleness.* (New; directly relevant to M6.)
   219	
   220	> "`sqlite3` against a mistyped path **creates an empty database** rather than failing, and every subsequent query then answers from an empty schema — which reads exactly like a stale pre-migration index."
   221	
   222	*Failure mode 6 — retrieval quality decays invisibly (s3-F26, re-verified).*
   223	
   224	> "a generated ambient-types file carried graph mass **0.24750** on the drifted index vs **0.13119** on a clean rebuild (~1.9×), and score **49.0** vs **27.0**. On the drifted index it took **60.7%** of an explore envelope and starved the file the agent had actually named by symbol, which rendered **251 chars of a 10,970 reservation**."
   225	
   226	**F5b — CONFIRMED — the detection mechanisms exist, and none of them can see this failure class.** <https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/guides/indexing/>
   227	
   228	The three layers are named, on the page, as covering exactly two windows: the debounce window and the server-not-running gap.
   229	
   230	> "1. **File watcher with debounced auto-sync (always on)**" — "A debounce timer collapses bursts of edits into a single sync." (default 2000 ms, `CODEGRAPH_WATCH_DEBOUNCE_MS`, clamped `[100ms, 60s]`)
   231	> "2. **Per-file staleness banner** — covers the debounce window"
   232	> "3. **Connect-time catch-up** — covers gaps when the MCP server wasn't running" — "a (size, mtime) stat pre-filter, then a content hash on the rest"
   233	
   234	The banner:
   235	
   236	> "⚠️ Some files referenced below were edited since the last index sync —their codegraph entries may be stale: - src/Widget.ts (edited 800ms ago, pending sync)For accurate content of those specific files, Read them directly.The rest of this response is fresh."
   237	
   238	**F6 — INFERRED — these layers cannot detect the F5 class.** Derived from F5 + F5b. All three are keyed to *mtime/content* of files on disk. The F5 failure is not a file that changed and was missed; it is a file that was correctly re-extracted, after which **cross-file resolution elsewhere in the repo became wrong** and nothing was left behind to revisit. A per-file "is this file pending sync" signal is structurally incapable of seeing it.
   239	
   240	**F5c — CONFIRMED — and the vendor says so, explicitly.**
   241	
   242	> "### `codegraph status` — decided: no drift metric
   243	> The issue asked whether `status` should surface divergence. Decision: **no**.
   244	> A drift number cannot be computed without the full rebuild it would be recommending, so anything cheap enough to run on `status` would be an estimate — and an honest estimate is not available."
   245	
   246	This sharpens the s3 gap ("`status` deliberately declines to report a drift metric") into the load-bearing sentence: **you cannot cheaply detect drift, and detecting it costs exactly the thing you were avoiding.**
   247	
   248	**F19 — CONFIRMED (cited) — the CLI-vs-daemon variant.** s3-F27: after adding a symbol, `codegraph query "metodoNuevo"` returned "No results found" and the database had 0 rows for it; issue created 2026-09-20, closed 2026-10-07. Because only the MCP daemon auto-syncs, a user-facing CLI over the same index is stale by construction — i.e. **"the cache" and "the index the reader sees" can be different objects**, which is the sharpest possible violation of "its absence must not stop anyone reading memory" (M6).
   249	
   250	**F20 — CONFIRMED (cited) — adoption failure is a failure mode too.** s3-F33: "58% of trials with graph access made zero tool calls" (arXiv 2602.20048). A correct graph that agents route around produces the same outcome as no graph, plus the maintenance and drift cost.
   251	
   252	**F21 — INFERRED — the repo has already paid this bill once.** `docs/KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:31-34`: *"Non-goals, stated up front because this repo has a track record of building graphs nobody queries (`loop/graph_memory.py`, 631 lines, no query code, `~/dev/seza/.ctxgraph/graph.db`, wired once, never touched again…)"*, and `:489-499`: *"Building it now would mean testing it exclusively against synthetic data … which is exactly the failure mode wave2 flags … : correct and unused."* That is F20 and F19 in this repo's own history.
   253	
   254	---
   255	
   256	## 5. What fits here — options, not a choice
   257	
   258	Machine as given: Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1 + FTS5, numpy, RTX 4070 Ti SUPER 16 GB. **Not installed: sqlite-vec, sentence-transformers, fastembed, tree-sitter.** The repo is 246 commits, 1446 tracked files, 123 `.py` files, `.git` 19 MB. `project-memory/` **does not exist yet**, so there are no real `covers:` values to size this against.
   259	
   260	The GPT is bad and most of these numbers are small enough that I would not build anything before the corpus exists. Options as required:
   261	
   262	**Option A — no code graph. Git on `covers:`.**
   263	*Needs installed:* nothing. *Rebuild:* n/a. *Size:* 0 (uses `.git`, already 19 MB). *Drift class:* none — `git log` reads the truth, not a projection of it.
   264	*Answers:* the line-75 check exactly, and "which entries cover the files I am about to edit". *Does not answer:* "what does this change transitively affect", "is this entry still true".
   265	*Measured here:* 0.00 s per path, 0.01 s for a full-history path scan of one directory (F12).
   266	
   267	**Option B — reuse `code_graph/code_graph.py` as-is, full rebuild, never incremental.**
   268	*Needs installed:* nothing (stdlib). *Rebuild:* **0.94 s** measured now (was 0.6 s at 110 files). *Size:* **2.6 MB** to stdout; no file written. *Drift class:* none by construction — deterministic, byte-identical, no incremental path, no DB.
   269	*Answers:* symbol-level and call-level change within `*.py`. *Does not answer:* anything about markdown; nothing about non-Python paths in `covers:`.
   270	*The trap:* `covers:` names *paths*, not symbols, so a rebuild is mostly wasted — and partial language coverage is a **silent partial answer**, which is F19's failure mode wearing a different hat. A graph that answers for Python entries and silently not for the rest is worse than no graph, because the miss is invisible.
   271	
   272	**Option C — adopt an external indexer (codegraph).**
   273	*Needs installed:* the codegraph binary (self-contained; bundles its own runtime and grammars, so the absent tree-sitter is not a blocker). *Rebuild:* ~100 s for 27k files; ~0.3–0.4 s incremental sync; 1.88–2.02 s full index on a 392-file repo. *Size:* GAP. *Runs locally:* yes, 100% local.
   274	*Answers:* F7's measured win — but on **source-code architecture questions**, not on markdown memory (F2, F14). *Costs:* the entire drift apparatus of §4, plus a background daemon and a writer lock, plus `--stdin | git diff` plumbing (F16).
   275	
   276	**Option D — adopt Serena (LSP, no index).**
   277	*Needs installed:* a language server per language. *Rebuild:* n/a. *Size:* no index. *Drift class:* none — queries the live filesystem.
   278	*Answers:* live symbol navigation. *Does not answer:* markdown semantics (F5 lists Markdown as supported, but a markdown file has few language-level symbols), call-graph edges it never promised, or anything offline. *Also the only option above that can read `.md` at all.*
   279	
   280	**Option E — git for the check, stdlib `ast` for the refinement.**
   281	*Needs installed:* nothing. *Rebuild:* bounded by the diff, not the repo — parse only the changed file(s) to map each diff hunk to its enclosing symbol. *Size:* n/a. *Drift class:* none.
   282	*Answers:* the line-75 check **and** symbol-level precision on `*.py`, because the work is proportional to what changed. *Does not answer:* transitive call impact, non-Python.
   283	
   284	Trade-off axis worth naming: **A and E cost nothing and answer the check; C buys a measured win on a question this store has not been shown to ask; D is drift-free but changes tool surface and dependency footprint; B is free and correct but answers a narrower question than the design's entries are phrased in.** I am not choosing, per the brief.
   285	
   286	---
   287	
   288	## 6. The specific question: does the line-75 drift check need a code graph, or is git on `covers:` enough?
   289	
   290	> `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:75` — "A script with no model call also marks a decision or fact in `INDEX.md` when its code changed with no earlier change to the entry."
   291	
   292	> `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:79` — "**Not decided yet (EJ decides):** … how the script tells that the entry changed earlier than the code …"
   293	
   294	### The check, decomposed
   295	
   296	Two primitives, both pure git, given what the design says `covers:` is:
   297	
   298	`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:50` — the entry header holds "`covers` (the code paths it is about)". **Paths, not symbols.** That single word decides most of this.
   299	
   300	1. *When did the entry last change?* → `git log -1 --format=%at -- project-memory/entries/<id>.md`. **0.00 s here** (F12).
   301	2. *Has a covered path changed since?* → `git log --since=@<t> --name-only -- <each covers path>`. **0.01 s** for a full-history scan of `ancient_games/` (F12).
   302	
   303	Compare the two timestamps. No parse, no resolver, no index, no model. Ordering a commit against an entry commit is a field comparison on data git already stores as the record of what happened — the one artifact in this system with no drift class at all, because it is not a projection.
   304	
   305	### The case **for** a code graph
   306	
   307	1. **Precision.** A one-line comment edit in a 400-line covered file is a change to that file; a symbol-level check would not flag the decision the file encodes. This is real, and it is the strongest argument. *But:* git gives the diff hunk; mapping a hunk to its enclosing symbol is a ~30-line stdlib `ast` walk over the **changed file only**. That is Option E — it buys the precision without a repo-wide graph.
   308	2. **Call edges — transitive meaning change.** If `journal.py` calls into `stages.py` and `stages.py` changes, an entry covering only `journal.py` is not flagged. A graph would flag it. **This is genuinely more capability than git on paths has.** *But it lands on the wrong side of the design's own line:* "does this entry still match the code" is the background subagent's job (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:70-73`, "it reports to EJ which entry, what the entry says, and what the code does instead"), not the model-free script's. A transitive check is the more expensive question, and the design already pays a model to answer it. Line 75 is the cheap ordering check.
   309	3. **s3-F4 — StateTape reads staleness off a code-graph diff with no model call.** Verified at source (F8, <https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36319>, 2026-09-28):
   310	
   311	> "The key idea of StateTape is to model the repository as a symbol-level code graph, whose dependencies and language rules expose which symbols a write can affect. Upon this graph, a tape marks the symbols each write changed, which turns staleness from an inference about text into an observation of the agent's writes."
   312	
   313	Two caveats that survive the citation. First, the abstract concedes a model in the loop for the residue: *"a small manager model settles what the write log cannot."* The model-free claim covers **nomination**, not adjudication — the design's line 75 is nomination-shaped, but so is the model half of StateTape. Second, and decisive for our case: **a write is the agent's own edit, in the current session, on files it just touched.** StateTape marks what the write log logs. The line-75 check is "the code changed at some point in the last four days and nobody updated the entry" — a historical scan over commits the agent did not make. Different problem. And StateTape's records cover **symbols by construction** ("Its unit is the symbol (a function, method or class), the smallest unit whose change alters meaning, not position", s3-F3) — which means s3-F4's applicability is a consequence of *how StateTape chose to scope its records*, not a general property of drift checks. If `covers:` named symbols, s3-F4 would apply. It names paths.
   314	4. **s3-F26's production incident** — a drifted index degraded retrieval. True, and it is evidence *against* the graph for this check, not for it: it is what you get when you introduce a stale projection to answer a question git answers exactly.
   315	
   316	### The case **against** — for git on `covers:`
   317	
   318	1. **The graph introduces a staleness class into a check that had none.** F5 is the best available measurement of exactly that class: 4.3% of distinct edges wrong, bidirectionally, silently, with a README promising the opposite — and, per F5c, **no cheap way to detect it, because detection costs the full rebuild you were avoiding.** For a check whose entire value is "is this entry still trustworthy", a 4.3%-and-silent oracle is a downgrade from git's exactness, not an upgrade.
   319	2. **The same vendor already ruled on this.** F5: *"The full rebuild is the wrong one here — converging would mean teaching sync to manufacture thousands of wrong edges. Left as is, deliberately."* So the incremental graph — the only form that makes the check cheap — is the form its own maintainers declined to converge, and the full-rebuild form is the form you do not need because git is already the rebuild.
   320	3. **The repo's own rule forbids the edge.** `docs/KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:45-50`: *"No node or edge type without a listed query."* The design lists exactly one query for this store, and it is an ordering check over covered **paths**. No listed query needs a symbol or call edge. `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:524-528` names the follow-on and its trigger in the same breath: *"The code import/call graph is deliberately not built. If Guard is ever asked 'does this edit reach a hub transitively through imports,' the follow-on is a `kind='imports'` addition to `edge` … This is not built now because no listed query needs it."*
   321	4. **The repo's own doctrine points the other way.** `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:355-366`, citing the evidence-KG skill's rule 9: *"Build a new graph from the whole corpus. Never incrementally patch a production graph unless the importer proves exact update and provenance-merge semantics … a full rebuild from an append-only log at K3's scale … is cheap enough that incrementality buys nothing."* The in-repo graph honours this and cannot drift (F18: deterministic, byte-identical, 0.94 s). So the repo already has the drift-free shape — and it still does not need it, because git is cheaper and exact.
   322	5. **The git-bound memory literature reaches the same ledger from the other direction.** s3-F13, <https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14390> (2026-07-15), abstract read 2026-10-12 — *"Why Git Is the Memory Solution for the Agentic Development Lifecycle"*:
   323	
   324	> "We argue memory should instead be git-bound -- built into the repository's version control, inheriting the guarantees the machinery struggles to construct: **ground truth from commits, freshness from rebuild**, verification from the merge, containment from review."
   325	
   326	Note two things. It is a *memory* paper, and it lands on git — and when it does want structure, it wants a **git-anchored** one: *"A router dispatches breadth to a git-anchored structural map, pointed lookups to confidence-gated episodes, and rationale to decision synthesis."* Same split as `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:70-75`: git decides the ordering, something more expensive decides the meaning.
   327	6. **When it did use structure anyway, it still fell back to git plus a parser.** s3-F22 (relore, Hugging Face): the tool *"had to finish with grep plus a Python AST scan."* The empirical shape of "git, then parse" is what real systems converged on.
   328	
   329	### Conclusion, and the flip condition
   330	
   331	**Git on the `covers:` paths is enough for the line-75 check as the design states it** — because `covers:` names paths, because git is the authoritative record rather than a projection of it, and because a code graph's two unique capabilities (symbol precision, call edges) either reduce to a parse of the changed file or belong to the model-assigned job on lines 70-73. The check's own open question at line 79 ("how the script tells that the entry changed earlier than the code") is answerable with two `git log` calls and 0.00 s, and does not need to become an index question.
   332	
   333	**Three named conditions would flip this** — stated as tests, in the idiom of `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md` §5:
   334	
   335	1. `covers:` starts naming **symbols** rather than paths (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:50`). Then s3-F4's mechanism applies and a parser is needed. Test: does any entry's `covers:` field stop being a path?
   336	2. The check changes from *"did the covered code change"* to *"what does this change affect"* — the `codegraph impact` / transitive question. Test: does the script need an answer about a file not on any `covers:` list? That is the `kind='imports'` trigger already written down at `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:524-528`.
   337	3. Call sites drift at a rate that makes path-level flags useless noise. Test: if >X% of flags are false positives from unrelated edits in large covered files, precision has become the binding constraint — at which point Option E buys it for free, from the diff.
   338	
   339	Per the brief: options reported, not chosen.
   340	
   341	---

    10	## Recommendation
    11	
    12	Keep the project's memory as reviewed markdown, version-controlled with the code, as the single source of
    13	truth: short typed entries (decision, fact, lesson, runbook) that each carry an id, kind, author, recorded and
    14	verified dates, the code paths or symbols they cover, their evidence, and explicit supersession links — never
    15	deleted, only superseded. One canonical cross-agent instruction file (`AGENTS.md`, with thin per-tool adapters)
    16	plus a small generated index and the assigned task's state file are the only things read at session start;
    17	everything else is fetched on demand by filename or text search, or by following links, and a retrieved
    18	statement is checked against its primary source before it determines an action. Staleness is computed
    19	deterministically from changes to the code an entry covers, not from age; contradicting entries are kept side
    20	by side, marked disputed, checked against code and evidence, and escalated to a human maintainer when code
    21	cannot decide; each writer gets its own file. SQLite/FTS, vector search and code graphs are permitted only as
    22	gitignored, rebuildable derived caches — no vector, graph, database, context window or agent-private store is
    23	ever the source of truth, and any new store must first answer a named, demonstrated failure of the
    24	markdown-and-search baseline on this project's own tasks.
